- **Mass Times app UI — the directory becomes a feature (2026-06-17).** Wired the app to the deployed Mass Times backend (`https://ember-mass-times.dpgu.workers.dev`): nearby list, native map, church detail, search, saved churches, crowd-correction (verify/suggest edit), and a personal Mass check-in log. All under `apps/app/src/features/mass-times/` + `src/lib/mass-times/`, reached from a card in the Explore featured carousel. See `docs/features/mass-times.md`. The non-obvious bits:
  - **`expo-maps` binds its native view at *module import*, not first render.** `AppleMapsView.js` runs `requireNativeView('ExpoAppleMaps')` at the top level (guarded only by `Platform.OS === 'ios'`). A static `import` from the screen would execute that on load — crashing even the *list* view in a binary built before the module was added. So the actual map lives in `NativeChurchesMap.tsx`, loaded via `React.lazy(() => import('./NativeChurchesMap'))` inside a `Suspense` + a class `MapErrorBoundary`, and `ChurchesMap` short-circuits to a fallback on web (`Platform.OS === 'web'`). The list path never pulls expo-maps; the map degrades gracefully until a native rebuild.
  - **On-device recurrence is zoneless, so "what's next" must evaluate in the *church's* wall clock.** `@ember/api`'s `expandService` anchors occurrences at UTC midnight and pairs each with the stored `HH:MM` verbatim (no tz math — the core path shares the church's zone). To pick "the next Mass" the client compares against `wallClockNow(timezone)` (built from `Intl.DateTimeFormat` parts in the UTC frame), not `new Date()` — otherwise a São Paulo 18:00 Mass viewed from another zone sorts wrong. `occurrenceInstant` + `wallClockNow` keep both sides in one frame; unit-tested in `schedule.test.ts`.
  - **FTS5 needs explicit prefix terms for search-as-you-type.** The default tokenizer matches whole tokens, so `q=Jos` returned nothing until `Joseph`. Added `toPrefixMatchQuery` in the backend (`churches/queries.ts`): Unicode-aware tokenize (`/[\p{L}\p{N}]+/gu`, so accents work) → each token quoted + `*` → `"jos"*`. Quoting also neutralizes FTS operator characters a user might type. Done in the backend (right altitude — all clients benefit); ships to prod via CD on merge.
  - **Favorites + check-ins persist as JSON blobs in the generic `preferences` KV table — no migration.** A shared `loadJson`/`saveJson` (`features/mass-times/persisted.ts`) backs both zustand stores; they hydrate in the root `_layout` boot effect alongside prefs/bible/catechism. Selectors that *build* a new array/object (`Object.values`, `filter`) use `useShallow`; a selector returning a raw state slice (`s.checkins`) does not (it's already referentially stable). The per-install `X-Client-Id` for writes is the same pattern — a UUID generated once and cached in the KV.
  - **The scraped source never touches this repo.** The São Paulo sample (146 churches, the deployed dataset) was produced by an out-of-band converter that maps the private scrape → `@ember/api` `ChurchDump` JSONL → `scripts/import.ts` → `wrangler d1 execute --file`. Per the pipeline contract, only the dump shape crosses the boundary; the converter and source data stay outside the public repo. Day codes `MTWHFS`+`U` → `BYDAY`; full-year weekly rows become structured `service` rrules, everything seasonal/holyday stays as raw `church_text`. (`wrangler d1 import` doesn't exist in wrangler 4 — it's `d1 execute --remote --file`.)
  - **expo-location/expo-maps are native modules → need `pnpm ios` to take effect.** List, detail, and search work in the current dev build with no rebuild (location degrades to a São Paulo vantage with a visible "use my location" affordance). The map and real GPS light up after a rebuild.

- **Mass Times round 2 — native feel + the check-in/reminder/attachment features (2026-06-17).** A native-feel audit + five follow-ups. The non-obvious bits:
  - **The check-in is not its own concept — it's a church check-in that completes the Mass practice.** Per the product call, "Mass attendance" shouldn't be a parallel tally. So the check-in now has a *kind* field (Mass / Confession / Adoration / Visit) + a note, and a Mass check-in additionally calls `useLogCompletion().mutate({ practiceId: 'mass', date: format(getToday(),'yyyy-MM-dd'), subId: 'default' })` (`@/features/plan-of-life`) — so it flows into streaks / plan-of-life. The Mass practice id is `"mass"` (manual completion; OF+EF share it); completions key by practiceId+date+subId and can repeat per day. There's a direct `logCompletion`/`toggleCompletion` path that bypasses the PracticeFlow — exactly what a quick check-in needs.
  - **Mass reminders ride the existing weekly-notification machinery.** Mass services are weekly (`FREQ=WEEKLY;BYDAY=…`), so each becomes a native `SchedulableTriggerInputTypes.WEEKLY` notification at (start − lead). Added `scheduleMassReminders`/`cancelMassReminders` to `lib/notifications.ts` (same data-tag-cancel pattern as practice reminders, tag `massReminderChurchId`); the feature computes slots from BYDAY (`massReminderSlots`, expo weekday = dow+1, lead crossing midnight rolls to the previous weekday). Enabled-state persists in the KV; the OS holds the recurring schedule, so no boot reschedule.
  - **expo-maps gives user-location + recenter for free; the marker callout doesn't.** `properties.isMyLocationEnabled` (the blue dot) and `uiSettings.myLocationButtonEnabled` (a *native* recenter button) need no custom UI. But `onMarkerClick` is **iOS 18+** only — so the marker-tap bottom card (a slid-up `ChurchListItem`) only appears on 18+; older iOS still shows the native title callout, and the list view always covers it. The card state lives in the wrapper (`ChurchesMap`), not the lazy native module.
  - **Photo attachments: compress on-device to fit the 1 MB cap, upload raw bytes.** `expo-image-picker` → `expo-image-manipulator` (new context API: `ImageManipulator.manipulate(uri).resize({width:1280}).renderAsync()` then `.saveAsync({compress:0.6, format: JPEG})`; only downscale, never upscale) → `uploadAttachment` POSTs the blob (`await (await fetch(uri)).blob()`) to the attachment route with the content-type + `X-Client-Id`, threads the returned key into `correction.attachmentKeys`. The native picker is import-safe but guarded with try/catch (absent until rebuild); upload errors go through the global mutation handler via `mutate` callbacks (not an unhandled `mutateAsync` rejection).
  - **Native feel = AnimatedPressable + the right haptic + entrance animation, everywhere.** Every raw `Pressable` → `@/components` `AnimatedPressable` (spring scale/opacity); `selectionTick` for filters/toggles, `lightTap` for contact/location, `successBuzz` for check-in/verify/correction, `mediumTap` for destructive. Lists wrap rows in `Animated.View entering={FadeIn.duration(200)} layout={LinearTransition…}`. `AnimatedPressable` works as a `Link asChild` child under React 19 (ref passes through `...props` to the inner animated Pressable — no forwardRef needed). The repeated chip became one `ChipButton` (`selected`/`soft`/`disabled`/`icon`/`dense`).

- **Mass Times round 3 — map-first layout + the blue dot fix (2026-06-17).** The screen now opens on a full-bleed map (the primary surface) with the controls floating on it as `GlassSurface` pills, and a list mode one tap away (`index.tsx` `MapView`/`ListView`). The non-obvious bits:
  - **The blue dot never appeared because we never *asked* for permission.** `properties.isMyLocationEnabled` only draws the dot once foreground location is granted, and `useDeviceLocation` only *checked* (never prompted) on mount — it prompted solely when the user tapped the small LocationBar. Fix: the map screen calls `location.request()` once on mount (ref-guarded; iOS shows the system dialog only the first time, later calls just read status), so opening the screen lights the dot and pulls real nearby results.
  - **Error surfacing, not swallowing.** The old `catch` logged a `console.warn` and silently kept the São Paulo default — so a denied permission or GPS failure looked identical to "haven't asked yet," hiding *why* there was no dot. Now only the dynamic `import('expo-location')` failure (module absent until rebuild) is soft; past that, a denied permission → `status:'denied'` (LocationBar offers `Linking.openSettings()`) and a real GPS throw → `error` string shown verbatim. Both the list (LocationBar) and the map (a glass banner, shown only when `error`/`denied`) explain the failure.
  - **expo-maps' `cameraPosition` is initial-only — recenter goes through the ref.** Updating the `cameraPosition` prop does **not** move the camera; the native view exposes an imperative `setCameraPosition` (on both `AppleMapsViewType` and `GoogleMapsViewType`). So `NativeChurchesMap` is a `forwardRef` with `useImperativeHandle` exposing `setCameraPosition`, and `ChurchesMap` holds the ref: the custom glass recenter button and the auto-center-on-GPS effect (fires once `status` flips to `granted`) both call it. We dropped the Apple/Google native `myLocationButtonEnabled` in favor of our own glass button so it matches the app's identity and sits clear of the tab bar — superseding the round-2 note that leaned on the native button.
  - **Floating controls need `pointerEvents="box-none"`.** A `position:absolute` overlay band over the map would otherwise eat map gestures across its whole bounding box; `box-none` on the container + inner rows lets pans fall through the gaps so only the actual pills/buttons capture touches.
  - **Suppress native MapKit chrome — we draw our own.** Leaving `uiSettings` off the Apple view fell back to MapKit defaults (compass / pitch / scale controls floating on the right edge — read as a stray "gear + chevron"). Since we supply a glass recenter + search, explicitly set `uiSettings={{ compassEnabled:false, myLocationButtonEnabled:false, scaleBarEnabled:false, togglePitchEnabled:false }}` (Google: also `mapToolbarEnabled`/`zoomControlsEnabled` false).
  - **Map controls are three glass buttons: back · list · filters (no search).** Search lives only in the list view's header. The map's top bar is `back` (`router.back()`), `list` (mode swap), and a `filters` button carrying a small accent badge when any filter is active.
  - **The filter is a bottom sheet, not inline pills — and asks the question that matters.** The old kind pills answered "has Mass *ever*?"; the real question is "has Mass *today*?". `MassFilter = { kind?, today, favoritesOnly }`: `kind` narrows server-side, `today`/`favoritesOnly` refine on-device (`hasServiceToday` expands from the church's wall-clock midnight so it stays true all day; favorites from the store). Sheet is `@expo/ui` `BottomSheet` (content-sized, `index={open?0:-1}`), same as `ConfirmSheet`.
  - **Pins mirror the cards: a `church` SF Symbol, a `heart.fill` for saved ones.** Per-marker `systemImage`+`tintColor` keyed off the favorites record (gold church / burgundy heart). `church` is an SF Symbol from iOS 16+ so it's safe at our 16.4 target.
  - **The entry point moved from the Explore carousel to the Search portfolio** — a full-width `WideShortcutCard` (spans exactly two grid tiles, same jewel gradient laid out horizontally) above the Pray section, since Search is where users go looking for a place/thing.
  - **One shared header unifies map + list (they were reading as two separate experiences).** `MassTimesHeader` (variant `glass` | `plain`) is used by both modes: same compact Junicode title (matching the church-card names — the centerpiece), same gold action cluster (back · filters · list/map toggle, + search on list), differing only in a `GlassSurface` bar on the map vs plain on the list. Replaced the four separate floating blobs (back/list circles + title pill + filter circle) and the segmented `ViewToggle` (deleted). Church names dropped to `$4` and `numberOfLines={2}` so long names wrap instead of truncating ("São Pedro e São…").

- **CTS Lives of the Saints imported as a book (2026-06-20).** `book/cts-lives-of-the-saints` — 75 short authored saint biographies from the Catholic Truth Society pamphlet series, scraped from ecatholic2000.com. The non-obvious bits:
  - **ecatholic2000's "saints" are several distinct collections, not one corpus.** The `saints.shtml` page is a nav layer; the only *unique* content is the ~100 `/cts/untitled-NNN.shtml` booklets (each a single authored CTS pamphlet). The A–Z "Saints Index" (`/saints/[a-z]list.shtml`) just re-points at those same booklets + outbound links — no hidden trove. Butler's Lives and the Pictorial Lives it also indexes are already in `content/books/`.
  - **Not every CTS booklet is a *life*.** Of 100 scraped, 25 are sermons (the Vianney set), Marian doctrine (Immaculate Conception, Assumption), novenas, or thematic meditations (the "St. Joseph's Virtues/Titles/Dignity" cluster) — excluded from a *lives* book. Curation rule lives in `content/_import/cts-saints/build_cts_book.py`.
  - **Raw scrape kept separately from the curated book.** `content/_import/cts-saints/` holds the faithful raw import (all 100, manifest, scraper) marked staging; the book reads a cleaned subset. Keeps the excluded 25 + provenance recoverable without polluting the corpus.
  - **The source is OCR with real defects — fix only the deterministic ones.** Body text is unproofed OCR. Two safe automated repairs at book-build: the site renders em-dashes as a literal `U+FFFD` + stray quote (25 cases, all pattern `word�'` → `—`), and trailing `****` rules cling to imprimatur/colophon lines. Everything else (sentence-level OCR slips, missing title-page author attributions — only 3 of 100 carry an inline `BY …`) still needs a human proofing pass before this is reader-grade. en-US only; no pt-BR yet.
  - **Pristine pass: conservative OCR proofread, not a rewrite (2026-06-22).** Audited defect classes first — crucial finding: the ~2,300 "stray single quotes" are *legitimate British quotation marks* (`'…,' said`), not errors; naively stripping them would corrupt the text. Real defects were modest. Did it in layers: (1) deterministic safe fixes (missing-space-after-period, lone `i`→`I`, `christian`→`Christian`); (2) a conservative LLM proofread — 15 parallel subagents over all 75 chapters fixing only objective scan errors (split words, garbles like "must hay been"→"have been", missing words) while preserving wording/archaic spelling/British quotes/litany formatting; (3) dash repair — the source renders em-dashes as both "word- word" and no-space "word-word"; converted via a function-word heuristic (`word-and/the/but/which…` is unambiguously an em-dash; no compound ends in those). **Gotcha:** the no-space pass corrupted multi-hyphen names (`Joseph-of-the-Mother-of-God` → `…of—the-…`); the fix is that a real em-dash is followed by a *space*, never `the-/and-/an-`, so revert any `—(and|or|an|the|by)-`. Verification guardrail: every file must stay within ±1.5% char-delta vs. the pre-pass version — a larger swing means an agent rewrote rather than corrected. All 75 passed. Still LLM-corrected, not verified against the printed originals (that's the source-PDF audit, deferred).
  - **Litanies were scraped as blank-line-separated paragraphs — reformat them tight.** `marked` runs with `breaks:false`, so single newlines collapse; litany invocations need two-space hard breaks to stack. `content/_import/cts-saints/fmt_litanies.py` (run in `build_cts_book.py` after the H1 fix) detects a run of ≥3 invocations, joins them with hard breaks, italicizes the response (`*pray for us.*`), splits OCR-mushed lines, and adds one `### Litany of <saint>` heading (or promotes an existing "Litany …" caption). A bare-comma or `etc.` line continues an already-open litany (the source elides repeated responses, esp. St. Jude). Files with no litany return byte-for-byte unchanged — so a full-corpus regen touches only the 4 litany chapters (Dymphna, Rita of Cascia, Peregrine, Jude).

- **Angelus pt-BR closing collect — aligned to the traditional formula (2026-06-23).** The final oration (`content/practices/angelus/flow.json`) was a hybrid that matched neither pt-BR form people actually pray by heart. Brazil has two live versions: the **modern liturgical** ("Derramai, ó Deus, a vossa graça em nossos corações… cheguemos, por sua paixão e cruz, à glória da ressurreição") and the **traditional** ("Infundi, Senhor, nós Vos rogamos, a vossa graça em nossas almas… sejamos conduzidos à glória da Ressurreição"). The file had "Derramai" (modern verb) bolted onto "Senhor" + "em nossas almas" + the literal-from-Latin "para que nós, que…" structure (traditional). Since the rest of this practice leans traditional (Latin present, genuflection rubric on the Annunciation/Christmas, "rezado de pé"), aligned to the traditional form (`infúnde` → "Infundi", `quǽsumus` → "nós vos pedimos"). Final wording per the owner's own recitation: "Infundi, Senhor, nós vos pedimos, em nossas almas a vossa graça, para que nós, que conhecemos pela anunciação do Anjo a encarnação de Jesus Cristo, vosso Filho, cheguemos por sua paixão e sua cruz à glória da ressurreição. Pelo mesmo Cristo, Senhor nosso. Amém." Only the closing collect changed; the V/R versicles already matched standard pt-BR usage.

- **Explore "scroll past everything and get stuck" = RN's `automaticallyAdjustKeyboardInsets` leaking a phantom bottom inset — removed from `ScreenLayout` (2026-07-11).** TestFlight report: scroll the Explore tab to the bottom and it sails past the last row into a black void; swiping back "doesn't move" (the void is taller than a screen, so a couple of swipes still show black and it reads as frozen); re-tapping the Explore tab recovers because the native tab button issues a scroll-to-top. Root cause: `ScreenLayout`'s ScrollView set BOTH `automaticallyAdjustKeyboardInsets` and wrapped itself in react-native-keyboard-controller's `KeyboardAvoidingView` (behavior "padding") — double keyboard handling. The RN prop is buggy on the new architecture: keyboard show events (the iOS 26 search tab's integrated field is the everyday trigger — it raises the keyboard while all tab ScrollViews are mounted; rn-screens gestures can also drive it) add `contentInset.bottom`, and the matching remove is dropped for views that are off-window when the keyboard hides (facebook/react-native#47731, #41397 are this bug's family). The inset persists and can stack across keyboard sessions, so the scrollable range extends screens past the real content. Fix: keyboard avoidance is the KAV's job alone; the prop is gone from `ScreenLayout` (a comment in the file warns against re-adding it). Rule of thumb for future surfaces: never combine `automaticallyAdjustKeyboardInsets` with a KeyboardAvoidingView — pick one owner per scroll view (`PracticeEditSheet` still has the combo; it lives inside a modal that unmounts on close, which is why it hasn't bitten there).

- **St Francis' Office of the Passion — the four seasonal parts were present but unreachable; now branched on season (2026-07-26).** `little-office-passion-by-s-francis-of-assisi` looked hour-aware but only its *first* part was: the `select on hour` covered "the three last days of Holy Week and week-days throughout the year," while the other four parts Francis arranged (Paschal, Sundays & principal feasts, Advent, Christmas–Epiphany octave) sat flat in the scroll below it. So praying it in Advent meant scrolling past the whole Paschal and Sundays sections to reach the right psalm — the content was all there, it just never dispatched. Restructured into an outer `select` wrapping the five parts, each carrying its own hour sub-select. The non-obvious bits:
  - **Part 3 isn't a season, it's a day-type — so the dispatch is compound.** Francis' rubric assigns those psalms to "Sunday and the principal festivities from the octave of Whitsunday until Advent and from the octave of the Epiphany until Maundy Thursday." Seasons alone can't express that, but `select` accepts an **array** `on` and joins values with `:` before `lookupMap`, dropping segments right-to-left until something hits. So `on: ["liturgicalSeason", "dayOfWeek"]` with keys like `ordinary:0` / `post-pentecost:0` picks the Sundays part, while a plain `easter` / `advent` / `christmas` key wins for the seasons that govern every day of the week — Easter Sundays correctly fall through `easter:0` → `easter` → Paschal, which is what Francis wants. Weekdays land on `default: "passion"`. **Gotcha:** `lookupMap`'s numeric-range matching (`"0-2"`) only fires for keys that parse as numbers, so compound keys must be enumerated exactly — no ranges in a compound map.
  - **The map has to cover both calendar forms.** `getLiturgicalSeason` returns OF ids (`ordinary`) or EF ids (`epiphany` / `septuagesima` / `post-pentecost`) depending on `context.liturgicalCalendar`, and the same Sunday resolves differently under each. The map lists the Sunday key for all five (`ordinary`, `post-pentecost`, `epiphany`, `septuagesima`, `lent`) so an EF user isn't quietly served the weekday part.
  - **No `as` on the selects, per corpus convention.** Without `as`, `overrideKey` defaults to the auto-selected id, so a user's manual override sticks *per resolved value* rather than globally — override the part in Advent and you aren't still locked to Advent psalms at Easter. Sharing one `as` across the parts would also be unsafe here: the Paschal part's hours differ (`terce-sext-none` is a single combined option, since Francis gives those three Hours one psalm), and an override id that doesn't exist among a part's options renders an empty branch.
  - **Two parts don't have seven hours, and that's the source, not an omission.** Paschal has five options (Terce/Sext/None share one body); Christmas–Epiphany has no hour select at all — its closing rubric says the psalm is said "at all the Hours."
  - **pt-BR added throughout** (143 localized nodes; previously only the 8 hour labels). Psalm verses follow the traditional Brazilian Catholic register (`vós`, Vulgate-based, matching the Douay the English renders), rubrics use the impersonal liturgical voice ("Note-se que…", "Diz-se…"), and citations use `Sl`/`Lm`/`Ex`/`Lc`.
  - **Still English-only in one sense:** sections 0–1 are typed `prayer` but carry Robinson's editorial provenance (manuscript attribution, Wadding, Celano) — they render as prayed body text at the top of the flow and duplicate the manifest's `history`. Left as-is here since it's orthogonal to this change, but it's a real primitives mismatch worth revisiting.

- **Citations inside prayed text: the `psalm` cento section (2026-08-04).** Reported as "doesn't make sense to have `Ps. 56: 1.` in the same style and emphasis as the text… very poor readability." The Passion office's psalms were authored as `prayer` blocks with the reference baked into the prose (`"Ps. 56: 1. Have mercy on me, O God…"` joined by `\n`), so Robinson's 1905 editorial apparatus rendered at the same size, weight, and colour as the words being prayed — a psalm read as a wall of reference numbers. Added a `psalm` FlowSection carrying `verses: [{ ref?, text }]`, mapped onto the existing `verses` primitive under a new `style: 'cento'`. The non-obvious bits:
  - **`ref` has to be localized, `num` doesn't.** The `verses` primitive's existing `num` is `string | number` because verse numbers are language-neutral — but a cento citation carries a book abbreviation that isn't (`Ps.` / `Sl`, `Lam.` / `Lm`, `Luke` / `Lc`). So `ref` is `LocalizedText` in the FlowSection, the engine localizes it, and preprocess drops `.primary` into `num`. Per-language punctuation convention too: English `Ps. 55:9`, Portuguese `Sl 55,9`.
  - **Inline lead-in, not the numbered gutter.** `VersesBlock`'s `numbered` style puts `num` in a 36px left column — sized for `12`, not `Ps. 56:1`. On a 430px screen a gutter wide enough for a full citation squeezes the prayed text into a narrow column. `cento` nests the mark inside `PrayerText` instead (the same pattern `ResponseMark` uses for ℣/℟), so the verse keeps full width.
  - **Justification pulls citations apart, and U+00A0 does NOT fix it.** Prayed text is justified, so the space in `Ps. 87:9` got stretched into `PS.    87:9` while neighbouring lines stayed tight. The obvious fix — a no-break space — changes nothing, because **CSS Text 3 lists U+00A0 as a word separator**, i.e. an expansion opportunity, right alongside U+0020. U+202F (narrow no-break space) isn't on that list, so it welds the citation together. Worth remembering for any fixed-width token living inside justified text.
  - **Both gaps needed it, not just the internal one.** Welding `PS. 87:9` together still left the *trailing* gap elastic, so on a short heavily-stretched line the verse started a visible indent further right than its neighbours — read as "unnecessary leading space" on some verses but not others. The separator between citation and verse is now U+2002 (en space): fixed width, not a justification opportunity, and wide enough to set the apparatus off. Rule of thumb: inside justified text, *every* space that isn't a real word gap has to be a non-separator space, or the justifier will find it. Verified in the running app — this class of bug is invisible to tests and to the JSON.
  - **The conversion is scriptable because the translation was line-aligned.** en-US and pt-BR were `\n`-joined with matching line counts, so a regex over the leading citation split 16 blocks into structured verses with both languages paired, verified to zero misalignments before writing. Citations spliced mid-line (`"They repaid me evil for good and Ps. 37:21 detracted me"` — one verse stitched from two places) stay in the prose; only the leading one is lifted into `ref`.
  - **`scripts/validate-flows.ts` keeps its own `KNOWN_SECTION_TYPES` set** — separate from `types.ts`. A new section type has to be registered there too or every use reports `unknown section type`. (Baseline for that script is currently 111 pre-existing errors across 106 files, mostly `manifest.flow must be a string path`.)

- **Auditing the other little offices for the same defects — mostly a false alarm, two real hits (2026-08-04).** After the Passion office cleanup, swept all 89 practices in `collection/little-offices` (and the whole practice corpus) for the same problems. Worth recording what *isn't* there, so nobody re-runs this:
  - **The cento citation fix has no other application.** Two independent detectors (line-initial citations, then any citation-shaped token anywhere in prayed text) found zero other practices. St Francis' Office of the Passion is the only cento in the corpus — every other office recites whole psalms continuously (`little-office-bvm-roman` Prime is just Ps 8 straight through), so there are no per-line references to lift out. The `psalm` section type stays a one-user feature until another cento is imported.
  - **`little-office-serotina`** had the identical defect to the Passion office: section 0 was a `prayer` carrying the editorial preamble ("It is the custom of the Servants of God…"), duplicating `manifest.history` **verbatim** — so the office opened with a paragraph of provenance in the prayer body face. Dropped from the flow (the script asserts the text is actually present in `history` before deleting, so this can't silently lose content). It now opens on "Kneeling" → sign of the cross.
  - **`little-office-bvm-roman`** had a different shape of the same disease: `"Little Chapter During Advent - Is 11:1-2\n<chapter text>"` inside one `prayer`, so the label rendered as prayed text. Worse, several blocks mashed *three* liturgical items into one block — antiphon + label + chapter — and one hid a Little Chapter at the end of the `Memento rerum Conditor` **hymn** (which is why the first pass missed it; the walker only looked at `type: 'prayer'`). Split into `subheading` + `prayer`, matching the 214 existing `la`+`en-US` subheadings in that file. Note `LocalizedText` doesn't declare a `la` key even though the corpus uses it on subheadings everywhere — the type is under-specified, the engine's `localize` handles it fine.
  - **Deliberately left alone:** the `as above` rubrics in `little-office-s-benedict` (7) and `little-office-holy-tear` (1). They're correctly typed as `rubric`, they abbreviate only the opening versicle (*Deus in adiutorium*), and every hour in those offices carries 2,000–4,800 characters of real prayed text — nothing like the Passion office's Sundays/Prime, where the *entire psalm* was the pointer. Also three "long prose at the top of the flow" hits (`b-niccolo-albergati`, `god-the-father`, `office-preparatory-prayers`) are false positives — genuine long prayed text (Latin hymns, *Aperi Domine*). Heuristics flagged them; reading them cleared them.

- **The Passion office's 13 empty hours — resolving Francis' cross-references into real text (2026-08-04).** After the seasonal split and the citation typography, the office still had a hole that no amount of styling would fix: **13 of its 27 hour-branches contained no prayable text at all**, only a pointer. Sundays/Prime rendered, in full: *Antiphon — Holy Mary. Psalm — "Have mercy on me, etc.—as above."* Two causes, and the seasonal split made the first one worse (see the earlier entry: what used to be a scroll-up in one continuous document became an unreachable sibling branch). Now every branch stands alone. The non-obvious bits:
  - **Two different kinds of pointer, needing two different fixes.** Ten branches said *"…etc., as above"* — the psalm exists in the office, just under a different part, so it's a deep-copy from a small library keyed by incipit (`have-mercy` = Ps 56 from Passion/Prime, `clap-hands` = Ps 46+95 from Passion/Vespers, `sing-canticle` = Ps 97 from Paschal/Matins, etc.). Four said *"…as it is in the Psalter"* (Ps 69 twice, Ps 12 once) — those were **never in the practice at all**, so they come from `content/bible/drb/psalms.json`, with pt-BR translated to match the office's register.
  - **The psalter's verse 1 is a superscription, not prayed text.** `drb` Ps 69:1 is "Unto the end, a psalm for David, to bring to remembrance…" and Ps 12:1 opens the same way before the words proper. Francis' incipits (*"O God, etc."*, *"How long, O Lord"*) start at the prayed text, so Ps 69 takes verses 2–6 and Ps 12 keeps verse 1 with the superscription stripped by regex. Blindly inlining a psalter chapter would have put a librarian's note in the middle of an office.
  - **Advent Vespers is a deliberate truncation, so the cut belongs in the text, not the rubric.** Its rubric said the psalm is prayed only as far as *"Let all the earth be moved,"* with the whole *"Bring your own bodies"* verse included. Once the psalm is shown inline, leaving that as an instruction sends the reader hunting for a verse that isn't there — so the verse list is cut at that point (asserted against the "Bring your own bodies" verse landing last) and the rubric trimmed to the part that still applies (the Gloria, and when the psalm is used).
  - **The office no longer opens with manuscript scholarship.** Sections 0–1 were `prayer` blocks carrying Robinson's provenance essay — Wadding, Celano, Assisi MS 338 — rendered in the prayer body face and duplicating `manifest.history` verbatim. Dropped (guarded by an assertion that the manifest really carries the text; **the guard fired on the first run** because the flow uses a straight apostrophe in "abbess'" where the manifest has a curly one, so the comparison now folds typographic quotes rather than being weakened). The 850-character preface rubric moved into a `collapsible` titled "How St Francis prayed this office". Net effect: the Part and Hour pickers now sit **above the fold on the first screen** instead of two screens down, and the whole office is 3.0 screens instead of 4.7.
  - **Guard against regression:** `passion-office-seasons.test.ts` now walks all 27 branches through `resolveFlow` and fails if any resolves to under 200 characters of prayed text. That's the check that would have caught this class of defect from the beginning — the JSON was always well-formed and every other test passed while half the office was unprayable.

- **What four review agents caught that tests, the validator and a corpus build all missed (2026-08-04).** Ran reviewers over the branch. Every one of them found something real, and two of the findings were bugs in the *tooling* rather than the output — the class a green suite can't surface:
  - **The translation applier walked past a whole node type.** It handled `inline`/`text`/`label`/`title`, but a `response` section keeps its text under `verses[].v` / `verses[].r`. Those were skipped — and the "fail loudly on anything untranslated" guard stayed silent, because **it only reports keys it recognises**. A guard can't complain about a node it can't see. 52 strings across three offices shipped in English inside otherwise-Portuguese offices (serotina's O Sacrum Convivium, its whole preces series, Requiem aeternam, Ave Regina Caelorum, the closing Adoramus te). If you write a completeness check, make it enumerate what it *found* and compare against a total, not just flag what it failed to match.
  - **`ref` was localized all the way to the renderer and then thrown away.** `types.ts` types it `LocalizedText` and `resolve.ts` localizes it correctly, but `preprocessFlow` collapsed it with `num: verse.ref.primary` — and `VersesPrimitive.num` is a single `string | number` shared by both columns. So all 294 pt-BR citations were computed, stored, and never rendered: in side-by-side the Portuguese column showed `PS. 55:9`, and tap-to-switch kept the English citation. Fixed by giving the primitive its own `ref?: BilingualText` and threading a `side: 'primary' | 'secondary'` argument through `BilingualBlock.renderText` so the cento branch can pick the matching citation. Existing `renderText` callers ignore the new argument.
  - **The "every branch has real text" test can't see a missing appendix.** Inlining the cross-referenced psalms copied the psalm *body* but left behind the Ascension versicles printed after it — `paschal/matins`'s rubric assigns those to Matins, Terce, Sext and None, and `passion/vespers`'s assigns its pair to every Vespers from the Ascension to Advent. Four branches (`paschal/terce-sext-none`, `sundays/matins`, `paschal/vespers`, `sundays/vespers`) silently dropped text Francis assigns to exactly the hour they serve. The 200-char thin-branch guard passed the whole time because a psalm body is long. Restored the rubric + versicles + governing rubric to each.
  - **A test-timeout fix that couldn't fire.** `asyncUtilTimeout: 10_000` was set above vitest's *own* per-test budget — no `testTimeout` was configured, so the 5000ms default applied. The ceiling was unreachable, and when a `findBy*` did blow through you got vitest's generic `Test timed out in 5000ms` instead of testing-library's element-not-found with a DOM dump: strictly worse diagnostics than the 1s default. Added `testTimeout: 20_000` so the library's message fires first.
  - **Pre-existing, found in passing:** `src/test/setup.ts`'s mocked `@expo/ui` BottomSheet gates on `props.isOpened`, a prop that exists on neither the real API nor any call site (all 22 pass `index`, where `-1` means closed). Every sheet has therefore rendered `null` in tests, so no sheet content was ever asserted. The mock now honours `index`.

- **Finishing the pt-BR pass: all 89 little offices, and the last review loose ends (2026-08-04).** The remaining two offices were the largest and hardest, and closing them surfaced one bug class worth naming:
  - **A homoglyph got into prayer text and nothing in the toolchain noticed.** Typing the Akathist's "omofórion" I produced `omофórion` — Cyrillic `о` (U+043E) and `ф` (U+0444) sitting inside a Latin word. It renders identically, passes JSON validation, passes the corpus build, and passes every test; it breaks search, hyphenation and screen readers. Added `scan_script`-style checking: any letter in pt-BR text whose Unicode name isn't LATIN/COMBINING is flagged. The corpus is otherwise clean (one legitimate `º` ordinal in `meditacoes-ligorio`). Worth running after any hand-typed content pass.
  - **The Mozarabic sources are genuinely corrupt in places.** `midnight-office`'s Oratio Dominicalis and several Clamores versicles carry scribal garbling no edition resolves (`de decora facinora`, `loca que a latiaque`, `unictis egri`). Translated for the sense the surrounding liturgy demands rather than inventing grammar for the corruption, and said so in the conversion script. Same for `ordo-quiete`'s `pertraxi fugam a me`.
  - **Gloria Patri normalized to the canonical wording.** `content/practices/glory-be` — the practice named for the prayer — has `Assim como era no princípio`, which is what a Brazilian Catholic recites; this branch had introduced `Como era no princípio` in 20 places. Checked attribution first: **all 20 were mine**, none pre-existing, so normalizing touched nothing outside this work. 22 occurrences corpus-wide, all consistent now.
  - **`psalm` reached the authoring tool.** `apps/workshop`'s flow editor validated the type but couldn't show or create it — `FlowPreview` fell to `default` and rendered `[psalm]`, and the node form had no case. Added the tree summary + colour, a `PsalmFields` editor (ref + text per verse, ref optional), and a preview that mirrors the app's cento style. Verified it adds no tsc errors: 45 before, 45 after.
  - **`aria-hidden` on the citation only works on web.** React Native flattens a nested `<Text>` into its parent's accessibility label, so on iOS/Android a screen reader would have spelled "PS. 55:9" before every single verse. Setting `accessibilityLabel` to the prayed text on the parent `PrayerText` suppresses it on both platforms.

- **Saints' Masses were showing one reading and no Gospel — proper slots have to be laid *over* the ferial ones, not swapped for them (2026-08-20).** St Bernard (Aug 20, memorial) rendered a first reading and then nothing: no psalm, no acclamation, no Gospel. Switching the celebration chip to the ferial showed all four. The cause was an all-or-nothing fallback in `sources/of/buildMassFlow.ts` — `f.readings ? f : temporal` — which borrowed the day's temporal formulary *only when the saint had no readings at all*. That's the wrong granularity for how the Lectionary actually works.
  - **The Lectionary gives saints slots, not sets.** A memorial normally takes the weekday readings; what the Missal prints for the saint is whichever slots are genuinely proper — for Bernard just the first reading (Eclo 15, "she will fill him with the spirit of wisdom"), for Jan 22 only a Gospel acclamation, for Apr 7 only a psalm. So `readings` being present says nothing about the set being complete. Corpus-wide: **57 of the 157 sanctoral cycle-sets are partial**, and across the 534 celebration-days of 2026 the old rule left **47 without a Gospel**; after the fix, 1 — `tempore.christmas.after-epiphany.monday`, a genuine content gap in the corpus, not a renderer bug.
  - **The fix is a per-slot merge, resolved once at build time.** New `lib/mass-of/readings.ts`: `readingSetOf` (formulary + cycle → set) and `resolveReadingSet` (proper slots over ferial). `MassContext` now carries a resolved `readings?: ReadingSet` instead of a `readingsFormulary: MassFormulary`, so the renderer no longer re-derives the cycle key in two places and can't disagree with itself between the Full-Mass and Readings-only views.
  - **The merge is silent, deliberately.** No "these are the ferial readings" rubric: the whole-set fallback was already silent, and a note on three of four slots would read as an apology for something rubrically ordinary. The `slotSource` provenance field (`proper` / `common:<key>`) stays untouched — it distinguishes the *Missal's own* offered choices, and ferial fill-in isn't one of them.
  - **Watch the negative control when a fallback is involved.** Stashing the fix made 11 tests fail, not 1 — the old renderer read a field the new context no longer sets, so everything crashed and the "does it fail without the fix?" question went unanswered. Reverting only the merge's last line (`if (ferial) return proper`) is the control that actually isolates it: exactly 1 failure.

- **The translation defect that survives five review passes: a term that carries an argument (2026-09-02).** Follow-up review of Ambrose's *On the Mysteries* pt-BR (#346, #347). The book had already been through five completeness passes and a mechanical audit — paragraph counts, chapter:verse numbers, Scripture book names, heading maps, diacritics — all clean, and clean again here. Three real defects were still in the text, and all three are invisible to every check of that kind, because **nothing is missing and nothing is misspelled**. Each is a word rendered plausibly in isolation that destroys a link the text depends on:
  - **A proof-text that no longer proves anything.** ¶9 argues *from a single word*: Genesis says the Spirit moved on the waters, David says the heavens were made "by the spirit of His mouth," therefore the Spirit was working, not merely present. Rendered "pelo **sopro** da sua boca" — which is what Ave Maria and Jerusalém print for Ps 32:6 and is not wrong as a verse — the quotation stops naming the Spirit and the next sentence ("cada uma dessas afirmações se apoia no testemunho do profeta") has no antecedent. The rule this yields: **when a Father quotes Scripture to make a lexical argument, the verse has to be rendered from the version the edition translates** (here Figueiredo, the Portuguese Douay counterpart), not from whichever modern psalter reads most naturally. The same sentence had "todo o seu **exército**" (Almeida's Hebrew "host") where the source says "strength" (*omnis virtus eorum*).
  - **An anaphora pointing at a word that isn't there.** ¶24 called Noah's dove "a **likeness** of this dove"; the translation had "uma **figura**." ¶25 then opens by objecting to that exact sentence — "how is it that we say that the **likeness** was there and the reality here" — which the translation rendered, correctly, "a semelhança." So the Portuguese reader is told "we say X" about something the text never said. Detection is cheap once you look for it: count each glossary term per paragraph in both languages and diff. `likeness` was 11 in en-US and 10 in pt-BR; everything else matched.
  - **A noun the translator supplied and the source withheld.** ¶38 "the pleasing comparison to **those that are shorn**" became "com as **ovelhas** tosquiadas" — then the next clause says "pois sabemos que **as cabras**…". The source is vague on purpose: the Vulgate's Song 4:1-2 has goats, so Ambrose's antecedent is goats, and the English translator kept it unnamed. Filling a pronoun's referent in is the most natural thing a translator does and the easiest way to introduce a contradiction two words later.
  - **What to actually run.** Word-count ratios per paragraph (0.83–1.05 here, all normal — they catch dropped sentences, nothing else) and verse-number diffs are necessary and cheap, but they were green on all three. The check that found them is a **per-paragraph term-frequency diff against the translation journal's own glossary**, plus reading each paragraph *against the one after it* rather than against its source line. Watch for substring false positives when you do — `figure`/`prefigurado`, `shadow`/`foreshadowed`, `semelhança`/`semelhante` all matched spuriously; anchor on word boundaries that include accented characters.
  - **Quote style is corpus-wide noise, not a finding.** en-US here uses curly quotes and pt-BR straight ones. Swept all 14 bilingual books: the split is roughly even and often disagrees *within* one book (`augustine-confessions` en-US 940 curly / 134 straight). Each import keeps whatever its source had. Don't "fix" it, and don't report it.

- **The glossary is the review instrument, and it has to be written down before it can catch anything (2026-09-02).** Sixth review pass on Ambrose's *On the Mysteries* pt-BR (#346, #347, #348). The previous pass's own prescription — a per-paragraph term-frequency diff against the translation journal's glossary — found three more defects on the first run, and five more surfaced once the glossary was extended to cover the terms it had not named. That asymmetry is the lesson: **the check is only as good as the glossary, so every defect fixed should be written back into the glossary table, not just into the prose log.** A term that was never listed is a term nothing can diff against.
  - **The gloss must contain the words it glosses.** ¶57 quotes Song 5:1 and the next sentence says "understand why He spoke of *meat and drink*." The Portuguese rendered the verse from the Vulgate (favum/vinum → "favo"/"vinho") and the gloss from the source ("alimento e bebida"), so the two words being explained did not appear in the quotation they explain. Same shape as ¶9's Psalm proof-text from the last pass, one paragraph later in the same book. **When a paragraph comments on the wording of a quotation, the quotation is not free to follow a different Bible than the gloss does** — and this is where a translator's instinct to reach for the familiar liturgical wording is most dangerous, because that wording is right everywhere except here.
  - **Check the chapter synopsis against the chapter.** These NPNF chapters open with an unsigned editorial summary, translated as body text. Chapter 7's summary said the Church calls herself "negra e formosa"; ¶35, which it summarizes, said "morena." The summary is the one place a term appears *outside* the paragraph that argues from it, so a synopsis/body disagreement is a free signal — and "morena" also flattened the black↔white antithesis the paragraph is built on ("Ela era morena — como se tornou agora, de repente, branca?").
  - **Two English words collapsing into one Portuguese word is as bad as one splitting into two.** "cleansed" (Naaman, ¶16-17) had become "curado," which is also "healed" (¶18, ¶22-24) — so the chapter's own distinction between being cleansed of leprosy and being healed at the pool disappeared, and the same English word was "purificado" in the parallel sentence at ¶19. Diff term frequencies **in both directions**: en→pt catches a term that lost its rendering, pt→en catches a rendering serving two terms.
  - **Number agreement carries argument too.** ¶37 rendered "your eyes are like a dove's" in the plural and then explained it in the singular ("à semelhança de uma pomba desceu") in the same sentence. The source is singular four times over because the point is the *one* dove at the Jordan.
  - **Watch for words the target-language Bible supplies for free.** ¶29's Psalm quote had gained "precioso" ("Como o óleo precioso sobre a cabeça") — standard in Brazilian psalters, absent from the source. Same failure as ¶38's "ovelhas" last pass: the translator is not adding a gloss, they are quoting from memory a verse the edition renders differently.
  - **Localizing a proper name is not altering the apparatus.** "Bosor" (¶36, the source's LXX spelling) → "Bosra" was flagged and left alone: it is the same city in the standard Portuguese biblical form, consistent with Naamã / Jerubaal / Melquisedeque. The fidelity rule is about chapter:verse citations and the wording of arguments, not about refusing to spell names the way the target language does.

- **`content/books/church-fathers/` is ~420 individual works deep, and only 2 had a pt-BR translation before this pass (2026-09-02, #351).** Each author/work pair under `church-fathers/` is its own book unit (own `book.json`, own `en-US`/`pt-BR` dirs) — nothing like a single "translate the Church Fathers" task exists; it's ~420 separate one-work translation jobs. Picked Ignatius of Antioch's *Epistle to Polycarp* (single chapter, ~1,350 words) as a scoped first pass. Confirms the pattern from the Ambrose/Augustine/Gregory Thaumaturgus translations (#343–#350): translate one short work end-to-end (translation + journal + `book.json` langs/TOC), review it clean, ship it as its own PR, rather than batching multiple works into one translation pass.
  - **A personal letter's "you" doesn't stay one person.** Ignatius addresses Polycarp individually through most of the letter, then pivots to the whole Smyrnaean congregation in Chapter 6 ("Give heed to the bishop, that God also may give heed to *you*" only makes sense as a command to the flock, not the bishop) and partway through Chapter 8. English "you" doesn't mark the switch; Portuguese tu/vós has to, so the address shift has to be worked out from context — bishop being told to do something personally vs. a plural imperative to a group — before translating a single-addressee letter that also has a congregation as its real audience. Documented as a translation-journal decision so a later review pass doesn't mistake the mixed register for an inconsistency.
  - **Three independent review passes on a 1,350-word chapter all came back clean** — no defects surfaced that the Ambrose/Augustine passes above needed five-plus rounds to find. Length is doing real work here: single-chapter, self-contained devotional letters are cheap to review exhaustively in one or two passes; the multi-round defect hunts upstream were all on much longer, argument-dense treatises where a single mistranslated word breaks a link between paragraphs pages apart.

- **A recurring wrong register is a better search key than a recurring wrong word (2026-09-02).** Seventh review pass on Ambrose's *On the Mysteries* pt-BR (#346, #347, #348, #350). The previous pass's fix — write every corrected term back into the glossary so the frequency diff can see it — worked: the extended glossary is what surfaced all three defects here, and the two structural sweeps it does not cover found nothing. What is new is the shape of the search that found the best of them.
  - **The same wrong register keeps pulling different words off the source.** Three separate defects across these passes are the *bread* register: "the body of life" → "o pão da vida" (pass 1), "meat and drink" → "favo/vinho" (pass 4), and now ¶47's "angels' **food**" → "o **pão** dos anjos" from the Portuguese psalter. Once a register has captured a translator twice, stop diffing word by word and **grep the whole chapter for that register** — every occurrence of pão/alimento at once — rather than waiting for the glossary to name the next casualty. A register is a much smaller search space than a vocabulary, and it repeats.
  - **A word that is right in the destination can still be wrong in the approach.** ¶47 holds one word steady and then swaps it: angels' *food* → that *food* → that *food* which you receive → "this living **Bread**." Naming the manna "pão" in the first clause is not a mistranslation of that clause — it is correct three sentences later. It just spends the contrast before Ambrose draws it, and strands "daquele alimento" pointing back at a phrase that no longer contains the word. **Where an argument turns on a word changing, check the clause *before* the turn, not the turn itself.**
  - **A term can collide with the paragraph's own subject.** ¶11's "tranquillity of **mind**" was "do **espírito**" in a sentence whose subject is the Holy Spirit descending — so the word read as the Spirit just named rather than as the faculty. A rendering can be defensible against the dictionary and still be unavailable *in that sentence*, because something else in it has already claimed the word. The frequency diff catches this as a count mismatch (mind 3/2); reading catches why it matters.
  - **Divine titles follow the source; proper names may be localized.** ¶6's "the angel of the **Lord Almighty**" (Mal 2:7) had become "o Senhor **dos exércitos**" — the standard Portuguese wording, and the Vulgate's own *Domini exercituum*, but not the title the edition being translated gives. That the Latin agrees with the target-language Bible makes the substitution more tempting, not more correct. This is the far side of the "Bosor → Bosra" line from last pass: spelling a name the way the language spells it is localization; **swapping one title of God for another is a change of wording, and the wording is what fidelity is about.**

- **Ignatius' *Epistle to the Trallians* translated to pt-BR (2026-09-02).** Fourth Ignatian letter translated (after Polycarp, Romans, Ephesians), continuing the one-short-work-per-PR pattern for `content/books/church-fathers/`. Single chapter, ~1,570 words. Three review passes: pass 1 found one defect (a bracket boundary cutting through the Portuguese contraction "do" — `[a cruz d]o Senhor` → `[a cruz do] Senhor`), passes 2 and 3 came back clean. Remaining untranslated Ignatian epistles: Magnesians, Philadelphians, Smyrnaeans (Martyrdom and the spurious epistles are separate, later works).

- **Post-merge review of the Trallians pt-BR translation found nine defects after three "clean" passes (2026-09-02).** The translation PR ran its own review three times and logged passes 2 and 3 as clean. A post-merge audit then found nine objective defects across three further rounds, and only stopped when two independent runs of pass 7 both came back empty. The interesting part is *which* checks found them.
  - **The mechanical categories were genuinely clean, every single time.** Section counts, bracket parity (15/15), parentheticals, Scripture localization, heading hierarchy, diacritics, `vós` conjugations, clitic placement, `book.json` — six passes, no defect in any of them, ever. **A translation review that only runs the mechanical checklist will report clean on a file with nine defects in it.** The checklist is necessary and it is not close to sufficient; every defect found here came from reading the text or from comparing it to something outside itself.
  - **Two independent reviewers disagreed, and the disagreement was the signal.** In pass 6 one run verified the file and pronounced it clean while the other, reading fresh with no knowledge of the prior passes, found two real defects. A clean verdict is evidence, not proof — it is worth about as much as the reviewer's angle of attack. Running two reviewers with *different mandates* (one adversarial-verification, one fresh-eyes) beat running the same audit twice, which is what passes 2 and 3 effectively were.
  - **Defects cluster where a diff cannot look: headings, pronouns, and collocations.** Of the nine, two were in chapter headings (editorial apparatus in a different grammatical voice from the body — a translator working inside the letter's "vós" voice drifts into it without noticing), two were possessives (`sua` marks neither person nor number, so every English "their" is a place the Portuguese can silently reattach to the nearest noun), and one was a reciprocal that quietly narrowed ("ame ao outro" for "love one another" — grammatical, and says something smaller than the source).
  - **The most dangerous false friend has the *correct* etymology.** "Sympathized in the joy" had become "se compadeceu da alegria". *Compadecer-se* and *sympathize* are the same word — both are literally "suffer with" — which is presumably exactly why it was chosen. But *compadecer-se* has narrowed in living Portuguese to *pity*, and pity is a word for suffering, so the sentence said Polybius pitied Ignatius' joy. Etymology justifies the calque and current usage refutes it; **check what the word does now, not where it came from.**
  - **Consistency defects are invisible from inside one file.** Three of the nine only appeared when the file was read against its siblings: a demonym ("esmirniotas") that the `epistle-to-polycarp` journal had explicitly ruled against, an identical English heading ("The same continued") rendered one way here and another in Ephesians, and a word ("privado") that the corpus already spends on a *different* English term ("deprived"). None is detectable by looking at Trallians alone, however carefully. **The sibling cross-check has to be a step in the review, not an afterthought — and a convention logged in one book's journal is invisible from inside another, so it has to be copied into the Key Terms of every book it governs.**
  - **Log the judgment calls you keep, not just the changes you make.** Three renderings were considered and deliberately left alone ("among any" → "entre alguns", "strangled" → "sufocados", "para com ele"). Two later reviewers independently re-flagged "entre alguns" and then found it already recorded — which is the entry doing its job. Without it, every future pass re-litigates the same three lines.

- **Ignatius' *Epistle to the Magnesians* translated to pt-BR (2026-09-02).** Fifth Ignatian letter translated (after Polycarp, Romans, Ephesians, Trallians), continuing the one-short-work-per-PR pattern for `content/books/church-fathers/`. Single chapter, ~1,650 words. Six review passes this time, not three, applying what the Trallians post-merge audit taught about where defects hide: pass 1 (bracket convention, found & fixed — `[so to do]` had been absorbed into the sentence with no bracket), pass 2 (fresh proofread + reciprocal/possessive/false-friend adversarial checks, clean), pass 3 (adversarial heading cross-check, found & fixed — Ch. 14's impersonal editorial heading "Your prayers requested" had drifted into Ignatius's own first-person voice, "Peço as vossas orações", the exact defect class Trallians pass 5 found in its Ch. 1 heading), pass 4 (structural re-verification, clean), passes 5–6 (diacritics/name-consistency sweep and a full verb-conjugation sweep, both clean — two consecutive clean passes to close). Doing the heading-voice check as its own dedicated pass, instead of waiting for a post-merge audit to find it, caught the defect before merge. Remaining untranslated Ignatian epistles: Philadelphians, Smyrnaeans (Martyrdom and the spurious epistles are separate, later works).

- **Post-merge audit of the Magnesians pt-BR translation (2026-09-02): six passes of counting missed twelve defects that five passes of reading caught.** The translation shipped with six pre-merge review passes logged, which found two defects (a dropped bracket, a heading that drifted into first-person voice). Four post-merge passes that read the two texts against each other clause by clause found twelve more, and it took until passes 11 and 12 to get two consecutive clean ones — and the split has nothing to do with effort or care. Passes 1–6 were all *inventory-shaped*: count the headings (16/16), count the bracket pairs (8/8), sweep the diacritics, sweep the plural-"vós" conjugations, check the scripture refs resolve. Every one of those checks passed on a file containing all twelve defects, because **every one of them was well-formed, correctly accented, correctly conjugated Portuguese sitting in exactly the right structural slot and saying the wrong thing.** An inventory pass cannot see a sentence that is complete and wrong; it can only see a sentence that is missing or malformed.
  - **The archaic auxiliary is a trap for translators reading modernized English.** Roberts-Donaldson's "Do ye all then... pay respect to one another" (New Advent modernizes it to "Do all then...") has *do* as the emphatic auxiliary and "pay respect" as the clause's only finite verb — the Greek has one verb, ἐντρέπεσθε, and nothing answering to ποιεῖτε. It had been read as lexical *fazer*: "Fazei, pois, todos, imitando a mesma conduta divina, **e** respeitai-vos uns aos outros" — an objectless transitive imperative plus an inserted coordinator that splits one English clause into two Portuguese commands. **Ungrammatical Portuguese survived six passes** because no pass ever asked whether a sentence parsed; they asked whether its pieces were present. When the source is a 19th-century translation, its archaisms are a defect class of their own.
  - **A false friend can invert a warning into a compliment.** "Since by your savour you shall be convicted" (ἐλεγχθήσεσθε — exposed, found out) had become "sereis **reconhecidos** pelo vosso sabor". *Reconhecido* is neutral-to-commendatory in Portuguese (*ser reconhecido por algo* = to be credited for something), so the sanction that motivates "Be salted in Him" read as praise. Fixed to *denunciados* — the standard idiom for a trait that betrays you (*o sotaque o denunciou*). Note this is the mirror of the Trallians *compadecer-se* finding: there the calque was too narrow, here too positive. **Both are cases where the sentence stays fluent and the polarity flips.**
  - **Unsourced insertions are not harmless pleonasm.** "contemplei **em vós**, nas pessoas antes mencionadas, toda a vossa multidão" added a locative the source does not have — and two adjacent unmarked *em*-phrases in apposition read as identifying "vós" *with* the persons before mentioned, dissolving the synecdoche the sentence exists to make (the named delegates stood in for the body Ignatius could not see). Likewise Ch. 7's "a um só **voltou**" for "has gone to one": doctrinally sound, but it asserts destination-identical-to-origin as a lexical fact where the English deliberately leaves each limb's "one" unspecified. **Adding a true proposition is still adding a proposition.**
  - **A fix can be the defect.** Pass 1's only job was restoring a bracket around "[so to do]", and it rewrote "daquele que assim o quis" as "daquele que **nos** quis [assim agir]". The bracket came back and the sentence stopped parsing — Portuguese *querer* does not license accusative-plus-infinitive with a distinct subject, and clitic climbing leaves *nos* governed by an intransitive infinitive that cannot take it. A later pass counted the bracket, found 8/8, and moved on. **A structural check will happily certify the damage a structural fix caused.** Verify a fix by re-reading the sentence, not by re-running the count that prompted it.
  - **Half of what a review pass reports is wrong.** Across four adjudication rounds, roughly half the candidates raised did not survive scrutiny — and the rejections needed the same rigor as the fixes. "Comungar convosco" was raised as a sacramental false friend and rejected because the Trent catechism has "comungam **na** mesma fé cristã" (same verb, same figurative sense, same regency, same head noun) and because sacramental *comungar* never takes a personal comitative in this corpus; both proposed replacements would have introduced *new* defects. **The grep is what separates a finding from a plausible story**, and an unadjudicated review pass applied wholesale would have damaged this file more than it healed it.
  - **The journal is not exempt from the review.** Seventeen journal claims had to be corrected across four rounds, and most of them were written *by this audit* — including, in the very bullet warning "assert a cross-book convention only with the grep in hand", the ungrepped assertion that *gozar* "occurs nowhere in the corpus except this file" (448 occurrences in Liguori, 16 in Montfort). A wrong journal entry is worse than no entry: the one claiming *gozar de* + person was the established sibling construction had it exactly backwards, and would have locked a deviation in place against the future review meant to catch it. **Scope every claim to the search you actually ran** — and grep your own notes as hard as you grep the text. The final audit sharpened this into a rule with a clean edge: across the whole journal, **every quoted string — Portuguese and English, in-book and cross-book — was verbatim correct, and every single failure was a count or a universal.** "Three named delegates" where the chapter names four; "every possessive *próprio*" against two counterexamples; "the corpus's dominant causal conjunction" when plain *porque* outnumbers it thirty to one. **Quotation is cheap to get right and easy to verify; quantification is neither. When a claim can be made by quoting instead of counting, quote.**
  - **Search the stem, not the citation form.** The *gozar* sentence had to be rewritten three times, and drafts 2 and 3 failed for the same mechanical reason: they grepped the literal string `gozar` and so never saw *goze*, *gozeis*, *gozei*, *gozaremos* — four occurrences across three sibling letters, plus personal-object uses in Montfort and Liguori that flatly contradicted the claim. In a heavily inflected language the citation form is the *least* common form in running text. Grep `goz[aeio]` and read the hits. Corollary: **when a claim has already been wrong twice, the next draft needs a narrower claim, not a better-worded one** — the version that finally held ("the corpus has a settled rendering for *this formula*, and this letter was the only departure") is a fraction of what the first draft asserted, and it was the only part ever supported.
  - **The process lesson, which generalizes past this book: more passes of the same shape do not converge.** Passes 4, 5 and 6 each came back clean and each raised confidence, but they were re-running the checks that had already passed — three clean passes in a row meant only that the file was still structurally sound, not that it was faithful. Two consecutive clean passes is a stopping rule that only works if the passes differ in kind. **A translation review needs at least one pass that reads the two texts against each other clause by clause with no checklist in hand, and that pass should come first, not last** — the counting passes are cheap, necessary, and never sufficient. Concretely, for the next translation: make pass 1 a clause-by-clause read, adjudicate every candidate against the corpus before touching the file, re-read each fixed sentence whole, and only then run the counting passes.

- **A grep's scope is part of its claim, and a suspiciously small result is evidence about the glob before it is evidence about the corpus (2026-09-03).** Pass 13 of the Magnesians audit — the pass that closed it clean — raised one candidate and rejected it, and the rejection turned on this. The candidate said Ch. 9's "aquele *a quem* eles justamente esperavam" was a regency error, since *esperar* takes a direct object or *esperar por*, never dative *a*; its evidence was "only two hits in the whole `content/books/*` corpus" for the *esperar* stem. Both hits were real and the corpus figure was fiction: the glob `*/*/pt-BR/*.md` matches a different set of files from every directory it runs in — from `content/books/` exactly one directory (`aquinas-opera-omnia/catechetical-instructions/pt-BR/`), from `content/books/church-fathers/` the eleven church-fathers works — and never the one-level books (Liguori, Montfort, Trent, Sales, Butler, Kempis, Morrow), which are the bulk of the corpus. Recursive `rg` immediately turns up "glória eterna a quem espera, e porque espera". **This is the pass-8 lesson recurring in its path form rather than its morphology form** — that one searched too narrow a *word* (`gozar`, missing *goze/gozeis/gozei*), this one too narrow a *tree* — and both wrote the result up as if it covered the corpus. Two hits for a common verb across a corpus this size is not a finding, it is a broken glob. (The linguistics also matter, and generalize: relative *quem* in Portuguese is **always** preceded by a preposition, and *a* is the default when the verb governs a direct object — the *objeto direto preposicionado*. "A quem" therefore says nothing about a verb's regency, and the corpus is full of it with verbs that take no dative: "*A quem buscais?*", "«A quem procuras?»", "aqueles a quem amamos", "o tirano a quem serves". A reviewer who reads "a quem" as dative will manufacture this same false positive on any transitive verb.)

- **Ignatius' *Epistle to the Philadelphians* translated to pt-BR (2026-09-03).** Sixth Ignatian letter translated (after Polycarp, Romans, Ephesians, Trallians, Magnesians), continuing the one-short-work-per-PR pattern for `content/books/church-fathers/`. Single chapter (Greeting + 11 chapters), ~1,580 words. Applied the lesson logged above for Magnesians directly — pass 1 was three *parallel independent* passes of different kinds (structural/mechanical, Portuguese-only proofread + cross-sibling term check, sentence-by-sentence fidelity) run together rather than in the sequence that took thirteen rounds last time, and each subsequent round paired a fresh fidelity read with an independent re-verification of every prior fix rather than repeating the same checklist. Five rounds (ten independent passes) to two consecutive clean, six genuine fidelity defects found and fixed, all of them fluent, correctly-accented, grammatically sound Portuguese that said something subtly wrong:
  - **A noun narrowed into a heavier one.** "secure your safety" → "assegurar a vossa **salvação**" (salvation) instead of **segurança**. The corpus already had this exact English word rendered correctly in `epistle-to-the-ephesians`; the fix was to match the existing precedent rather than re-derive one.
  - **A verb whose direction reversed.** "**detects** the secrets [of the heart]" (the Spirit perceives hidden things) → "**revela**" (discloses/publishes them) — the mirror image of the Trallians/Magnesians false-friend pattern (a word whose meaning has drifted), except here the two English senses aren't even close: perceiving and publishing are opposite directions of information flow.
  - **A preposition that changed what was governed.** "believe **in** love" (love as the object of belief) → "credes **com** amor" (love as manner: believe lovingly). A one-preposition swap that is easy to type without noticing and changes the theological claim.
  - **A quantifier's scope collapsed.** "if **either** of such persons **do not** speak" (distributive — triggers per-teacher) → "se **nenhum** destes falar" (collective — triggers only if neither speaks), letting a teacher who stays silent about Christ escape the judgment as long as the other one doesn't.
  - **A name collided with a common word.** "Burrhus" → "**Burro**" (the Portuguese word for "donkey"). Two sibling journals (`epistle-to-the-ephesians`, `epistle-to-the-magnesians`) already document and reject this exact rendering for this exact name — a **convention violation invisible from inside the file**, caught only by the cross-sibling check, same failure mode as the Trallians "esmirniotas" defect.
  - **An unmarked addition that changed the claim.** "a man of **reputation**" → "homem de **boa** reputação" — inserting an evaluative word the source doesn't have, unbracketed, in a text that otherwise brackets every interpolation.
  - **Two later candidates were raised and correctly dismissed as non-defects**, both idiomatic elaboration with no propositional-content change ("da boca de" for "from [a man]," "por espírito de contenda" for "out of strife") — two independent reviewers converged on dismissing them using the same standard ("does this change what the sentence asserts?"), which is the calibration line the review philosophy already states (objective defects only, not style) applied concretely.
  - Remaining untranslated Ignatian epistles: Smyrnaeans only (Martyrdom and the spurious epistles are separate, later works).

- **Gregory Thaumaturgus' *On the Trinity* translated to pt-BR (2026-09-03).** A ~1,070-word fragment (Salmond's Ante-Nicene Fathers translation), one of the many single-chapter individual works nested under `content/books/church-fathers/` that each carry their own `book.json` and can be translated independently of the larger corpora sitting alongside them (`aquinas-opera-omnia`, `church-fathers` itself, `catholic-encyclopedia`, `douay-rheims-challoner` — thousands of files each, out of scope for a single translation pass). Three passes, all reported clean on the first try: a clause-by-clause fidelity read, a mechanical structural check (sentence/em-dash/parenthesis counts matched exactly, 40/10/5 both sides), and a book.json/TOC/heading + diacritics check. **All three clean verdicts were wrong** — a post-merge review found six defects; see the correction entry below.
  - **Preserved two source peculiarities rather than "fixing" them.** The English already pairs "on the mount" with a citation to Matthew 3:17 (the Jordan baptism), which reads like a conflation with the Transfiguration (Matt 17:5, same words "This is my beloved Son", actually on a mountain) — kept the citation exactly as the source has it, since correcting an inherited citation isn't the translator's job. Also left "haines subsistent" as "nomes subsistentes" ("names subsistent"), reading "haines" as an OCR artifact of "names" — no other word fits the sentence.
  - **When picking a monolingual work to translate, a directory without its own `book.json` is a collection, not a book** — `church-fathers/`, `aquinas-opera-omnia/` and similar author directories hold dozens to hundreds of individually-catalogued works one level down, each small enough to translate and review in one sitting. Worth checking `find content/books -iname book.json` before assuming a top-level directory name is the unit of work.
- **A "clean on the first try" verdict is a symptom, not a result — and it cost six defects in *On the Trinity* (2026-09-03).** The entry above claimed three clean passes on a ~1,070-word fragment. A post-merge review of the same file found six objective defects, every one of them fluent, correctly-accented, grammatically plausible Portuguese. The mechanical numbers that earned two of those three clean verdicts were all *genuinely* correct and stayed correct through every fix — 40/40 sentences, 14/14 italic spans, 10/10 em-dashes, 5/5 parentheses, 15/15 semicolons, five inline Latin terms byte-identical, H1 matching the TOC. That is the whole lesson: **the counting passes were not wrong, they were irrelevant to the defects that existed.** Every one of the six was a sense defect inside a structurally perfect sentence, and no count could ever have surfaced one.
  - The six, by failure mode: an **elliptical noun mis-attached** ("I see in all three essentials" → "Vejo em todos os três o essencial", reading *all* as modifying *three* and handing "os três" an antecedent that appears two sentences later); an **honorific inverted into a hedge** ("near successor of the apostles" → "quase sucessor", i.e. *almost* a successor — proximity adjective read as an approximation adverb); an **article that re-parsed as a preposition** ("são chamadas **a** Santíssima Trindade" = called *to* the Trinity); a **demonstrative agreeing with the predicative instead of its antecedent** ("estes são também nomes subsistentes" after "Todas (as pessoas) ... são chamadas"); an **adversative turned causal** ("but" → *pois*, in a file where the other five instances are all *mas*); and an **adjective coordinated with an adverb** ("inseparável e ilimitadamente" for "inseparably and without limit").
  - **Three of the six are the same defect wearing different clothes: a function word whose grammatical role flipped.** *Quase*, the article *a*, *pois*, and the *-l/-lmente* ending are the smallest tokens in their sentences, they are invisible to a fidelity read that is tracking propositional content clause by clause, and each one changes what the sentence asserts. The Philadelphians entry above logged "a preposition that changed what was governed" as one defect among seven; here that shape is the *majority* of the findings. Worth promoting to its own checklist line: **after the content read, re-read the file for function words alone** — prepositions, articles, demonstratives, conjunctions, degree adverbs — with the English clause beside each one.
  - **The stopping rule from the Magnesians entry held exactly as written, and this run is its confirmation.** "Two consecutive clean passes only works if the passes differ in kind" — the original three passes were one fidelity read plus two counting passes, so the rule was never actually satisfied, and the entry's "all clean" read as convergence when it was a single unreplicated fidelity read plus two passes that could not have caught anything. This review reached clean with two consecutive clean passes of genuinely different kinds: (1) a mechanical re-verify *plus* an independent adversarial bilingual clause-by-clause read, and (2) a **monolingual** pt-BR proofread that deliberately never opened the English, paired with a recursive cross-sibling convention check. The monolingual pass is the one worth institutionalizing — reading the target text with no source in front of you is what makes "inseparável e ilimitadamente" audible as broken Portuguese, whereas with the English beside it the eye confirms *inseparably* and moves on.
  - **The independent verification pass earned its keep on the one defect the first-pass reviewer could not see.** Five of the six were found by the reviewer's own clause-by-clause read; the sixth ("quase sucessor") was found only by a fresh reader who had not just spent an hour forming a mental model of the text. Its own report also mis-stated its italic-span count as 13 while enumerating 14 — the Magnesians lesson recurring verbatim, that **quotation is cheap to get right and quantification is neither**, this time in a reviewer's output rather than in the journal. The finding was still correct; the count was still wrong; and the fix was to trust the independently-greppable number over either reader's tally.
  - **What did not change: the imported source.** The two documented peculiarities of the Salmond text (the Matthew 3:17 citation paired with "on the mount", and "haines subsistent") were left exactly as they are, and `en-US/ch001.md` was not touched at all. A review of a *translation* has no licence to edit the public-domain source it was translated from, and New Advent was unreachable from this environment anyway (the egress proxy blocks the domain) — which is a second, independent reason to leave an imported text alone rather than "correct" it from memory.

- **A second review round on *On the Trinity* found two more defects, and both were the failure mode the first round had just named (2026-09-03).** The prior entry ended by promoting "re-read for function words alone" to its own checklist line, on the evidence that three of six defects were function words whose grammatical role flipped. Running that line as an explicit instruction — rather than as a hope that a fidelity read would catch them — found two more in the same 1,070-word file, both pronouns pointing at the wrong antecedent: `visto que **este** está muito distante da natureza humana` (Portuguese *este* takes the nearest antecedent, *objeto corpóreo*, so the clause asserted the corporeal object is what is far removed from human nature — the inverse of the argument, contradicted by the very next sentence; English "that" is distal and reaches *a spiritual object*, so *aquele*), and `é eterno **consigo mesmo**` (*si/consigo* is obligatorily bound by the clause subject, asserting the Word is eternal *with itself*, while the coordinated *é inseparável dele* is non-reflexive and must then point elsewhere — a split referent the English "Himself"/"Him" pair does not have).
  - **The lesson is about the checklist, not the file.** Eight defects have now been found in this one short fragment across two rounds, and none of the review passes that found them were new inventions — they were the passes the accumulated journal already prescribed, run deliberately. What changed between the round that reported "clean on the first try" and the rounds that found eight defects was not reviewer skill but whether the checklist was executed as a checklist.
