A named failure mode is worth re-running as its own pass **even on a file that was just corrected for that exact failure mode** — especially then, since a file freshly edited for demonstratives is a file whose demonstratives were recently disturbed.
  - **Reflexive vs non-reflexive is a pt-BR-specific trap with no English counterpart, and belongs on the checklist by name.** English lets "with Himself" stay ambiguous between the subject and a co-referring divine person; Portuguese does not — *consigo* forces subject-binding, *com ele* forecloses it. So a construction that is faithfully ambiguous in the source cannot be faithfully ambiguous in the target, and the translator must pick. The tell is a coordinated pair where one member is reflexive and the other is not (*consigo mesmo* … *dele*): that split is almost always an artifact of translating each conjunct separately, and almost never what the source asserts.
  - **Two reviewers disagreed, and the adjudication pass was what resolved it.** The monolingual reader called *consigo mesmo* a defect; the bilingual reader had explicitly cleared it as faithfully mirroring the English's own reflexive/non-reflexive split. A third reader, given both arguments and asked only to rule IN or OUT, ruled IN on the grammatical point (obligatory binding) that neither had stated decisively — and simultaneously ruled OUT two other candidates the same readers had raised (`nomes são estes` for "names are such as these", and the reduced clause `por ser espiritual`). **Disagreement between review passes is signal, and the cheap way to use it is a third pass that adjudicates rather than re-reviews** — it converges in one round, and it protects against over-editing as much as under-editing. Both dismissals were independently reconfirmed by the next round's fresh bilingual reader, who reached the same verdict on `por ser espiritual` without being told it had been raised.
  - **A fix can introduce a convention defect while correcting a fidelity defect.** The first draft of the *consigo mesmo* fix wrote *com **E**le mesmo*, a reverential capital that would have been the only one in `church-fathers/*/pt-BR` — all 25 mid-sentence divine pronouns across the sibling translations are lowercase (`dele` ×12, `nele` ×13), including this file's own *dele* three words later in the same clause. Nothing in the fidelity reading was wrong; the capital was simply a house-style decision made silently while thinking about grammar. **Grep the siblings for the convention before committing a fix that touches orthography or capitalization**, and re-run the verification pass on the post-fix text rather than on the text the passes actually read.

- **The third review round on *On the Trinity* found the first defect no fidelity reader could have found: the source's typesetting (2026-09-03).** Two rounds of meaning-level review had left `fala assim**:—**` untouched, because at the level those passes work, it is correct — the Portuguese says what the English says. The defect is that the colon-plus-em-dash is a 19th-century English typesetting convention, and Portuguese does not stack the two marks. The numbers: unspaced `:—` appears in 479 `en-US` files and 10 Latin files under `content/books/` and in no pt-BR file but this one; *paragraph-final* colon-dash appears in 233 `en-US` files and, in pt-BR, on exactly one line in the whole corpus — this one. **A translation inherits its source's punctuation conventions silently, and nothing in a bilingual read will flag them, because faithfully mirroring the source is exactly what the defect looks like.** The pass that catches this is the corpus-wide grep for a mark's distribution by language, not any reading of the text.
  - **The corrected grep is a different finding from the first grep.** The monolingual reader reported that `:—` appears in *no* other pt-BR file and concluded pt-BR never uses the construction. Re-running it found the opposite: 22 pt-BR files do use a colon-dash, always *spaced* (`: —`) and always mid-line introducing speech that continues on the same line. Had the original claim gone unchecked, the fix would have been argued from "pt-BR never does this" when the truth is "pt-BR does this, differently, and never in this position" — same verdict, different and much stronger reason. **A reviewer's grep is a claim, not a measurement, until you re-run it**; the Magnesians lesson (quotation is cheap, quantification is not) applies to a reviewer's `grep -c` as much as to their prose.
  - **Structural parity is a check for dropped content, not a rule that punctuation must be transplanted.** Fixing this made the em-dash count 9 (pt-BR) against 10 (en-US), breaking an invariant three prior rounds had verified at 10/10. That is correct: the nine mid-sentence parenthetical dashes still pair 1:1, and the tenth English dash is a typesetting mark with no pt-BR counterpart. When a mechanical invariant and a language convention conflict, **the invariant is the thing that yields** — but say so explicitly in the journal, or the next round will "restore" it.
  - **Adjudication ruled three of four candidates OUT, and that is the pass working, not failing.** The same three-reader-plus-adjudicator shape as the previous round, run again, dismissed `*o homem*` (italicizing the article is required in pt-BR for a contrast English gets from bare-vs-definite syntax), `ele se iguala` for "He supplies in the same equality (*aequiparat*)" (Salmond's "supplies" is his own strained rendering of *aequiparat*, which is why he glossed it; a bestowal reading would assert subordinationism), and an alleged *cauda pronominal* in `a quem podemos considerá-lo semelhante` (no doubling — `-lo` is the object of *considerar*, `a quem` the complement of *semelhante*; the English does the same with two distinct pronouns). **A round that rejects most of what it raises is cheap insurance against over-editing**, which on a settled text costs more than under-editing.
- **Ignatius' *Epistle to the Smyrnæans* translated to pt-BR (2026-09-03) — all seven authentic Ignatian letters now have a pt-BR translation.** Last of the seven (after Polycarp, Romans, Ephesians, Trallians, Magnesians, Philadelphians); only the *Martyrdom* and the *Spurious Epistles* under the same author remain English-only, and those are separate, later, non-authentic works, not part of this set. Single chapter (Greeting + 13 chapters), ~1,900 words. Three review rounds (seven independent passes), the by-now-standard shape: round 1 as three parallel independent passes (structural/mechanical, Portuguese proofread + cross-sibling term check, sentence-by-sentence fidelity), found four non-overlapping defects; rounds 2–3 (two passes each, a fresh cold read plus an adversarial re-verification) both clean.
  - **A locative preposition can quietly drop the subject as agent.** "that all righteousness might be fulfilled **by Him**" (Christ himself accomplishing it, part of a list of things Christ truly *did*, anchoring the anti-docetic argument) had become "para que **nele** se cumprisse" ("in Him", a passive event happening within him). One preposition, and Christ goes from actor to venue in a sentence whose entire point is what he actively did in the flesh.
  - **A doubled negative can invert its own escalation.** "not only **not** receive, but... not even meet with" is an English idiom that intensifies (don't receive them; worse, don't even encounter them). Translated as literally as English word order suggests — "não deveis não somente receber" — Portuguese reads it backwards: "não deveis" placed before "não somente" scopes the outer negation over the whole clause and inverts which action is forbidden. Fixed by fronting "não só" ("não só não deveis receber... nem sequer encontrar"), the construction already used correctly elsewhere in the same corpus (`epistle-to-the-magnesians` Ch. 1). **An idiom that intensifies in English can flip polarity in Portuguese if translated clause-shape-first instead of meaning-first.**
  - **A recurring formula already has a corpus answer — check before coining a new one.** "and all **by name**" (closing salutation) was rendered "nominalmente", a real Portuguese word but one that skews toward "in name only / nominally" — a false-friend-adjacent near-miss, not an outright error, so grammar and dictionary checks both pass it. `epistle-to-polycarp` already renders this exact recurring Ignatian closing phrase "**pelo nome**". Same failure mode as Trallians' "esmirniotas" and Philadelphians' "Burro": a defect invisible from inside the file, caught only by checking the siblings for a phrase that recurs across the set.
  - **A bracket boundary can still split a contraction even when it matches the source's own boundary.** The source itself brackets "[a true belief in] Christ's passion" with the boundary falling mid-phrase; translating that boundary literally put the closing bracket inside the Portuguese contraction "na" (em+a) — `crença n]a paixão`. The fix wasn't to abandon matching the source's bracket span, just to slide the preposition "a" out in front of it so the whole contraction stays inside: `retornem a [uma verdadeira crença na] paixão`. **Matching a source bracket's span and keeping every target-language word intact are two different constraints, and either one alone is not enough.**
  - This chapter contains the earliest datable occurrence of the phrase "the Catholic Church" (Ch. 8, καθολικὴ ἐκκλησία) — rendered "a Igreja Católica", the fixed modern Portuguese term rather than a literal "universal/geral" gloss, since the phrase is already a proper term in the target language too.
- **Pseudo-Hippolytus' *The Apostles and the Disciples* translated to pt-BR (2026-09-03).** ~940 words, single chapter, mostly a bare list of apostles/disciples with their preaching field, see, and death — proper names and place names carry almost the entire fidelity risk here, not prose register. Four passes: a bilingual clause-by-clause fidelity read (round 1) found and fixed four proper-name inconsistencies against the stated "transliterate, don't correct" rule — `Allanum` had silently lost a letter (`Alanum`), `Carchedon in Gaul` (v18) and the separately-spelled `Carthage` (v19) had both collapsed to `Cartago` erasing a distinction the *source itself* keeps, and `Odyssus`/`Olympus` had been swapped for their scholarly/biblical name forms (`Odessos`, `Olimpas`) instead of transliterated as spelled; a mechanical structural count and a monolingual pt-BR-only read (rounds 2–3) both came back clean (81/81 numbered items, 3/3 headings, 8/8 italics, 86/86 paragraphs, correct diacritics throughout).
  - **A clean mechanical + monolingual pair is not the same as a clean fidelity pair, and this file is proof in miniature of the `On the Trinity` lesson two entries above.** Deliberately running one more sense-level bilingual re-read *after* two clean passes — specifically hunting for fluent-but-wrong sentences rather than repeating the count — found two false-friend/calque defects neither prior pass had a way to catch: "the assumption of Christ" (v13) had gone in as *a assunção de Cristo*, a correct cognate that is nonetheless the wrong Portuguese word here — *Assunção* is a term of art reserved for Mary's bodily Assumption, and Christ being taken up to heaven is *Ascensão* — and "they were honoured to preach" had gone in as *foram honrados **a** pregar*, a preposition calqued straight from the English infinitive construction that isn't idiomatic Portuguese (*ter a honra de* + infinitive is). Both read as perfectly grammatical Portuguese in isolation, which is exactly why the structural count and the monolingual read — neither of which had the English in view to compare against — passed them both.
- **The Didache translated to pt-BR (2026-09-03) — 20 defects across 7 rounds, and the same liturgical-creep bug caught twice in one prayer.** ~3,080 words, single chapter, 16 numbered sections — one of the earliest post-apostolic texts, picked after a survey of every `book.json` in the corpus lacking `pt-BR` turned up `content/books/church-fathers/` and `aquinas-opera-omnia/` as ~500 individually-catalogued small works each, not two undifferentiated giants (confirms the lesson already logged for Ignatius/Gregory Thaumaturgus). Seven review rounds (14 independent passes: alternating bilingual clause-by-clause and monolingual-only reads) found and fixed 20 genuine defects before reaching two consecutive clean rounds.
  - **The familiar liturgical Pai Nosso wording crept into this file's own Lord's Prayer twice, in two different rounds, and the first fix didn't inoculate against the second.** Round 2 caught "as in heaven, so on earth" rendered in the *familiar Missal's* earth-then-heaven order instead of the source's own heaven-then-earth order — flagged explicitly in the journal as "the one place the standard liturgical wording crept back in." Round 5, three rounds later, caught a second instance in the *same prayer*: "bring us not into temptation" (an active image, ≈ Latin *ne nos inducas*) had gone in as "não nos deixes cair em tentação" — the passive "let us not fall" image that is, again, the standard Brazilian Missal wording, not this specific 19th-century English edition's own wording. A translator (or reviewer) who has already spotted and fixed one liturgical-creep instance in a prayer does not thereby become immune to a second, independent instance of the identical failure mode three sentences later — every clause of a well-known prayer needs the same "is this the source's wording or the wording I already know by heart" check, not just the clause a prior round happened to flag.
  - **Two negation-reversal defects were both archaic-English parsing failures, not Portuguese failures.** "shall pay the penalty, why he received and for what" (Ch. 1) and "lest ever they shall fear not God" (Ch. 4) are both awkward, compressed 19th-century constructions where the plain sense is easy to get backwards on a fast read — the first was softened from "penalty" to "will render an account," the second collapsed a double negative ("cease to fear," i.e. *stop* fearing) into a single one that said the opposite (servants should *not* fear God). Both defects were fluent, grammatical Portuguese; both were found by a bilingual pass reading the archaic English slowly enough to parse what it actually says, not by anything checkable from the Portuguese side alone.
  - **A clitic pronoun's antecedent can drift mid-paragraph even when the paragraph gets the same construction right elsewhere.** Ch. 13's "dai-**o** aos pobres" used a masculine singular clitic for "as primícias" (fem. pl.) — the *same* paragraph correctly uses "dai-**as** aos profetas" a few sentences earlier for the identical noun. Grammatically each clitic looks locally plausible in isolation; only a pass that tracks the antecedent across the whole paragraph catches the drift.
  - Full defect roster by kind: 2 liturgical-wording-creep instances (Ch. 8, one prayer, two rounds), 2 archaic-English negation/sense reversals (Ch. 1, Ch. 4), 1 collapsed divine-title distinction ("Master" vs. "Lord" both flattened to "Senhor," Ch. 10, fixed with a new term "Soberano" since "Mestre" was already claimed by the corpus for "teacher"), 3 non-idiomatic calques/awkward nominalizations (Ch. 1 "para quê" as a noun, Ch. 4 "proferir o domínio", Ch. 4 "para dar" misread as a purpose clause), 2 regência slips (Ch. 4 "participante *em*" instead of *de*, Ch. 4 "aproximar-se *para*" instead of *de*), 1 clitic gender-agreement slip (Ch. 13), 1 tu/vós register slip inside a single sentence (Ch. 4), 2 capitalization inconsistencies (lowercase "igreja" once among four capitalized instances; lowercase "ação de graças" once among five), 1 lexical house-style miss ("David" vs. the corpus's own 213-to-23 "Davi"), and 2 minor unmarked additions/parallelism breaks (Ch. 11, Ch. 16) fixed for exactness rather than because they were actually misleading.
- **Gregory of Nyssa's *On Pilgrimages* translated to pt-BR (2026-09-03).** ~1,575 words, single chapter, one unbroken paragraph — Gregory's famous letter arguing that a pilgrimage to Jerusalem confers no special grace and can be a moral hazard for monastics. Chosen this run from a much larger backlog: of 420 individually-tracked works under `church-fathers/` (each its own `book.json`, translatable independently of the larger corpora sitting alongside them), 405 are still English-only — this is one of them. Three review rounds, all independently clean on first pass (no fix round needed): round 1 covered completeness (exact term-occurrence parity for God/Deus, Jerusalem/Jerusalém, Christ/Cristo, Holy Spirit/Espírito Santo, the 7-item vice list), diacritics, and divine-pronoun capitalization; round 2 covered mechanical punctuation counts, full proper/place-name parity (13 names), and verb mood/tense fidelity in the exhortative close; round 3 added a cold monolingual pt-BR-only proofread and a logical-connective pass (Now/Ora vs. Agora, Whereas/Ao passo que, Inasmuch as/Visto que, etc.) plus a firstly/secondly/thirdly enumeration check against the source (the source itself only has secondly/thirdly, no firstly — correctly mirrored, not an omission).
  - **Divine pronouns stay lowercase mid-sentence — confirmed again, this time by the reviewer rather than just asserted by the translator.** The `On the Trinity` journal established the convention (mid-sentence "ele"/"dele"/"nele" lowercase, capitals only where sentence position calls for it); this file's round-1 reviewer was explicitly told not to trust the translation journal's claim and to re-derive it from `ignatius/*/pt-BR` and `ambrose/mysteries/pt-BR` directly — it held up. First draft of this file had briefly used a capitalized "nEle" and "-Lo" (the only such instances anywhere in `church-fathers/*/pt-BR`), caught and fixed before the review rounds even started by cross-checking sibling files.
  - **~~A three-clean-round result on a well-chosen short text is a real signal, not luck.~~ It was luck, and it was wrong.** The bullet above originally argued that this letter's discursive prose, low proper-name density, and absent citation apparatus explained why three rounds came back clean where denser fragments in the same batch needed fix rounds. A post-merge `/review-book-translation` audit took **four more fix rounds and seven rounds in total** to reach a genuinely clean result, finding **sixteen** objective defects in the file those three rounds had passed: twelve in the first fix round, two more that surfaced only on a re-audit *after* those twelve were fixed, one introduced by a fix, and one more after that. The genre reasoning was a rationalization of a null result, constructed after the fact to explain why the reviewers found nothing — the correct reading of three clean rounds on a first-draft translation is that the rounds were weak, not that the text was easy.
  - **Naming an idiom in a review report is not the same as checking it, and the report format actively hides the difference.** Round 2 of the pre-merge review wrote: *"false-friend idiom check (licence, conductor, yeoman's service, waggon) — all correct senses."* Of those four, only *licence* was right. `conductor` had gone in as *guia* (a route-guide, when the source's conductor is the man who physically lifts the woman on and off her horse), `yeoman's service` as *penoso serviço* — an outright **sense inversion**, since the idiom (Hamlet V.ii) is commendatory and means good, faithful service — and `waggon` as *carro*, which unmarked reads as "automobile" in modern pt-BR. A checklist rendered as prose in a summary is indistinguishable from a checklist actually executed; the four hardest idioms in the letter were the four listed as verified. **Demand the quoted target-language rendering next to each idiom in a review report, not a verdict over a list of them.**
  - **The fixed-idiom trap: a correct word-for-word mapping can be captured by an idiom in the target language.** "who have made it a part of their devotion to behold those spots" went in as *que fizeram parte de sua devoção contemplar…*. Every word maps correctly, but *fazer parte de* is a fixed Portuguese idiom meaning "to **be** part of", so the clause first parses as "who **were** part of their devotion" and the intended causative reading is only recoverable on a second pass. Fixed to *que incluíram em sua devoção contemplar…*. This is the mirror image of the *Assunção*/*Ascensão* false friend two entries above: there a single word was wrong, here every word was right and the **collocation** was wrong — which is harder to catch, because there is no individual token to look up.
  - **A fix round changes what the next reviewer can see, so re-audit after fixing rather than declaring victory.** The two defects found in the second fix round (`among them` rendered as *ali*, relocating Gregory's charge from Jerusalem's inhabitants to Jerusalem itself; and "the **Dispenser** of my life" rendered with *dispor de*, the fixed idiom for "have at one's disposal") were both pre-existing — neither was introduced by the first round's twelve fixes. They surfaced only once the louder errors around them were gone. **Twelve defects found is a signal that the file is bad, not that the file is now clean; the round after a big fix round is the one most worth running, and it is exactly the round a translator is most tempted to skip.**
  - **Adversarial reviewers disagree with each other, and "cleared by name" carries no weight.** Across seven rounds this file collected three opposite verdicts on the single phrase "by postal conveyance": cleared by name as standard *cursus publicus* historiography, then flagged as an anachronism substituting a service for a vehicle, then accepted-with-reservations. The same pattern hit `conductor` and `yeoman's service`, both of which a round explicitly listed as verified before a later round found them wrong. **A review report's clean verdict on a specific item is evidence about that reviewer, not about the text** — which is the whole reason the fix loop has to keep running fresh rounds until one comes back clean *with nothing left to fix*, rather than stopping when a report sounds thorough.
  - **An omission repaired in the wrong slot is a new defect.** Restoring the dropped "to live" produced `fui colocado, para nele viver, pelo Dispensador de minha vida` — which inserts the purpose clause between the passive verb and its agent phrase, letting `pelo Dispensador…` be misparsed as modifying `viver` instead of `fui colocado`. The English keeps the agent adjacent to the verb ("placed **by the Dispenser of my life** to live"). Caught by the monolingual reviewer, which is the right instrument for it: the defect is invisible against the English (the content is all present and correct) and only shows up as a Portuguese parsing ambiguity. **Every fix round is also a new draft, and needs review of its own.**
  - **A cold monolingual reviewer cannot adjudicate a third-person hortative, and will confidently propose a regression.** The monolingual agent flagged `Ouçam de mim minha justificativa` as a *vocês*-imperative breaking the letter's otherwise consistent `tu`/`vós` register, and proposed `Ouvi` (vós). Applying that would have introduced a real defect: the English is "**Let them** hear from me my plea" — spoken *about* the objectors, not *to* the reader — so the third-person `Ouçam` is correct and `Ouvi` would have changed the referent. But the observation underneath the bad fix was sound: the bare form really is ambiguous, and this file marks its other hortatives with *Que* (`Que nosso próprio caso… não cause`). Resolved as `Que ouçam de mim…`. **Take a monolingual reviewer's report of ambiguity seriously and its proposed fix not at all whenever the fix turns on what the source says — by construction, it hasn't read the source.**
- **Gregory Thaumaturgus' *Canonical Epistle* translated to pt-BR (2026-09-03).** ~1,700 words, single chapter, 11 numbered "Cânones" — the earliest surviving canonical epistle in Church history, addressing penitential discipline after Gothic/Boradic invasions of Pontus, c. 254–258. Picked from the `church-fathers/` backlog (still ~400 English-only works after this pass) as a well-scoped single-work pick, reusing the "Gregório Taumaturgo" pt-BR author name already established by this author's `On the Trinity` translation. Four review rounds (8 independent passes: alternating structural/bilingual/monolingual), 8 genuine defects found and fixed, closing on two consecutive clean rounds (3, 4).
  - **A regência-only fix to a bad verb choice can cement the underlying error instead of surfacing it.** Round 1 corrected "convicted him of going" → "convenceu **de** andar" to "convenceu **a** andar" purely on preposition-government grounds, without questioning whether "convencer" (persuade) was the right verb for "convicted" (proved guilty of) — a forensic/evidentiary sense, not a causal one. The grammar fix made the wrong reading *more* fluent, which is exactly why round 2's bilingual pass had to catch it, not round 1's regência check: a regência check is orthogonal to a lexical-choice check, and passing the former says nothing about the latter. Fixed to "provou que ela andava".
  - **The same archaic-double-negative failure mode as the Didache — "shall not X and pay no heed" cancels to an affirmative obligation, and translating it as one flat negation flips the sense.** "You shall not see your brother's ox... go astray... and pay no heed to them" contains two negatives (shall-not + no-heed) that cancel to "you must attend to them," but the first-draft Portuguese ("e não lhes prestarás atenção") kept only one negation, asserting the reader *will* ignore them — directly contradicting the very next clause ("but you shall bring them back"). A monolingual reviewer with no access to the English correctly flagged the resulting Portuguese as internally contradictory, without knowing *why* — sufficient to locate the defect even though the reviewer's own diagnosis undersold it as ordinary incoherence rather than a dropped double negative. Fixed with a "não... sem" construction that correctly encodes the cancellation: "sem lhes prestar atenção; antes, sem falta, os trarás de volta".
  - **A cold monolingual reviewer re-finding an already-resolved item, across two independent review rounds, is confirmation the call was right — not noise to re-litigate.** Round 3's monolingual pass (run with no access to the journal) independently re-derived three items rounds 1–2 had already checked against the English and left unchanged (an Exodus quote's singular-to-plural drift, a present/present-perfect tense mix, and a single question mark spanning two rhetorical clauses) — a different reviewer, working from the Portuguese text's own internal logic alone, arriving at the same "this looks wrong" flags. That convergence is itself evidence the "not a defect" calls are genuine invisible-without-the-source mirrors of the archaic English rather than a first reviewer's blind spot: a call that survived only one check would be weaker than one two independent cold reads have now flagged and two independent bilingual checks have now cleared. The same round also surfaced two brand-new items this way — an odd biblical idiom ("after the eyes of fornicators") and two distinct words for what reads as one building ("oratory"/"temple") — both likewise confirmed as exact mirrors of the English on the first check, not translation defects.
- **Gregory Thaumaturgus' *On Matthew 6:22-23* translated to pt-BR (2026-09-04).** A ~256-word fragment (Salmond's Ante-Nicene Fathers translation) — the shortest work picked from this backlog to date. Single chapter, single unbroken commentary paragraph on the "single eye" saying. Picked from the `church-fathers/`/`aquinas-opera-omnia/` backlog (488 individually-catalogued works still English-only across the two trees as of this pick) as the third work by this author, reusing the "São Gregório Taumaturgo" pt-BR author name already established by *On the Trinity* and the *Canonical Epistle*. The whole homily turns on a wordplay ("the **single** eye is the love unfeigned") that has to survive translation intact — rendered as "**singelo**" and checked for consistency between the quoted verse and the commentary paragraph in every review pass, since a term drifting between the two would silently break the pun without breaking any grammar. Three review passes (a manual checklist pass plus two independent subagent passes, one of them adversarially primed to distrust the prior "clean" verdict and specifically re-derive the wordplay-consistency and journal-accuracy checks from scratch), all clean on first try — the short length likely mattered here as much as any process improvement: a single paragraph leaves little room for the drift, omission, and cross-reference defects that accumulate across the multi-page Ignatian and Didache translations logged above. One unrelated pre-existing defect surfaced incidentally by the third pass — the en-US source's line 5 read "But **it** your eye be evil" (an OCR "if" → "it" typo, present before this translation work) — deferred at the time as out of scope for a translation-only change, then **fixed in the follow-up review pass (2026-09-04)** to "But **if** your eye be evil". The correction was made without reaching the New Advent original (egress-blocked), on internal evidence alone, and that evidence is worth naming because it is the kind that *does* license a source edit: "it" cannot introduce a conditional clause in any English, the immediately parallel clause reads "if therefore your eye be single", Matthew 6:23 is "But if thine/your eye be evil" in every edition, the pt-BR translator had already silently read it as "se", and a corpus-wide grep found the string exactly once (so it is an isolated defect, not a house convention). Contrast the Catecismo Romano audit logged above, where "cross-verification against the source is essential, not optional" — that rule governs cases where two witnesses *disagree on a plausible word* and either could be right; it does not govern a reading that no edition attests and that is ungrammatical on its face. Still worth a separate source-correction pass across `church-fathers/`, since OCR digitizations of this vintage are likely to have more of these.
- **Aquinas' *Letter on the Manner of Studying* (to John of Vercelli) translated to pt-BR (2026-09-04).** A ~200-word letter — the "sixteen precepts" for study — the first pick from the `aquinas-opera-omnia/` half of the backlog rather than `church-fathers/` (still ~495 untranslated works across both trees; the pick avoided the tiny author/collection index stubs like `gregory-nazianzen/letters` and `apocrypha/gospel-of-nicodemus`, which are TOC placeholders with no real body text, not translatable works). Unlike every prior pick in this series, the canonical source here is **Latin** (`la/ch001.md`), not English — `book.json` lists `en-US` as a secondary standard public-domain translation, so the pt-BR text was translated from the Latin directly and the English consulted only for register (e.g. confirming "Lord of Hosts" for *Domini Sabaoth*), per the skill's "never translate from a translation" rule. Preserved Aquinas's *cellam* / *cellam vinariam* wordplay (monk's cell → wine-cellar, a Song of Songs 2:4 allusion) as "cela" / "cela do vinho" rather than reaching for unrelated Portuguese words for the two senses. Three review passes (manual checklist, then two independent re-reads focused respectively on translation-journal/book.json parity and phrase-by-phrase accuracy against the Latin), all clean — plausible for a single three-paragraph chapter with no headings, footnotes, or scripture citations to misalign.
- **Julius Africanus' letter *Africanus to Origen* translated to pt-BR (2026-09-04).** A ~500-word letter (Crombie's Ante-Nicene Fathers translation, catalogued under `church-fathers/origen/` since it addresses Origen) arguing that the Susanna episode is a later Greek addition to Daniel, not part of the Hebrew original — the pun ("holm-tree"/"saw asunder", "mastich-tree"/"rend asunder") only works in Greek, which Africanus takes as proof the passage wasn't translated *from* Hebrew. Kept the four transliterated Greek puns (*prinos*, *prisein*, *schinos*, *schisthenai*) untouched in italics, since translating them away would erase the argument they're evidence for. Three review rounds (6 independent passes, alternating bilingual and cold monolingual): round 1's monolingual pass flagged "nunca por inspiração profética" as self-contradictory against the preceding list of prophetic forms — verified against the en-US source, which has the identical construction verbatim ("never by prophetic inspiration" right after "by visions, and dreams, and an angel appearing to him"), so not a defect, just the source's own contrast between mediated revelation and the sudden ecstatic seizure described a sentence earlier. Rounds 2–3 both came back clean with no regressions, closing the review on two consecutive clean rounds.
  - **A monolingual reviewer's "this sentence contradicts itself" flag is exactly the kind of finding that must be checked against the source before it's treated as a defect — even when the logic reads as genuinely broken in isolation.** The English original itself contains the same apparent contradiction; a "fix" here would have "corrected" a faithful mirror of the canonical text into something the author never wrote.
- **Post-merge review of the *Africanus to Origen* pt-BR translation found three defects the pre-merge review had closed as clean (2026-09-04).** The translation shipped in #398 after three review rounds that closed on the "two consecutive clean rounds" rule; a post-merge re-review found three real defects, and **round 2 had specifically inspected two of them and pronounced both correct** — it re-verified the phrase "parceira de seu trono" as "correct" and raised the "Ora" vs "Mas" connector only to dismiss it as a stylistic preference. The three: (1) **"parceira" → "parceiro"** — Portuguese must commit to a gender where the English "the wife of their king Joakim, **whom** the king of the Babylonians had made partner of his throne" leaves it open, and the feminine made the *wife* the one elevated to the Babylonian throne; the referent is Joakim (the Greek relative is masculine accusative ὅν, the allusion is Jehoiachin's elevation in 2 Kings 25:27–30, and Africanus's next sentence — "if it was not this Joakim... whence had a captive such a mansion?" — is only an argument if Joakim is the one enthroned). (2) **"Ora" → "Mas"** before "todos os livros do Antigo Testamento" — the English's second "But" is the hinge of the whole letter (the pun works in Greek but not Hebrew — *but* the OT was translated *from* Hebrew *into* Greek, so the passage cannot render a Hebrew original), and "Ora" flattens it into a topic shift while duplicating the "Ora" already opening the previous sentence. (3) **"citado a outrem" → "citado outrem"** — a Hispanicized personal "a" that invites a dative reading ("quoted *to* another"), inverting "quoted from another word for word".
  - **The "two consecutive clean rounds" stopping rule measures reviewer agreement, not correctness — and reviewers agree most readily exactly where they are most likely to be jointly wrong.** All three misses share one shape: the **English is ambiguous or merely idiomatic and the Portuguese is forced to commit** — a gender English leaves open, a connector whose adversative force English carries in a word Portuguese splits in two, a verb whose object marking differs between Portuguese and Spanish. At those points "checked it against the source, it matches" is too weak a test, because several Portuguese renderings match the source's *words* while only one matches its *sense*. Worth adding as an explicit checklist item for future translations: **enumerate every place the target language must resolve something the source left unresolved, and justify each choice from context or history — not from the source's surface form.**
  - **Corollary to the journal's earlier note that a cold monolingual reviewer re-finding a resolved item is confirmation the call was right.** That holds — but only for items where the Portuguese *mirrors* the English. It does not extend to items where the Portuguese made a choice the English never made: there the monolingual reviewer has no way to see the defect at all (the text reads perfectly well in Portuguese — it is just about a different person), and the bilingual reviewer sees a phrase that "matches" and moves on. Both misses here were invisible to *both* review modes for that reason, which is why they survived three rounds. Catching them needed a third question neither pass asks by default: *where was the translator forced to choose, and is the choice right?*
  - **Audit the translation journal against the text, not just the text against the source.** The same post-merge review found a false claim in the book's own `translation-journal.md` Key Terms table — a row asserting the pt-BR text renders "rend asunder" as a verbatim "rasgar ao meio", a string that occurs nowhere in the chapter (it has "rasgado ao meio" in the narrative and bare "rasgar" in the sound-alike list, the latter faithfully mirroring the source's own bare "rend" there). It survived every pre-merge round because each round checked the *translation* and read the journal as a record of it rather than as a claim to verify. That is backwards for the one reader who matters most: the next translator of an adjacent work, who reuses these rows as settled term choices. Verifying that each Key Terms row's Portuguese actually appears in the chapter is cheap (one grep per row) and now belongs in every review round — and a row describing a term that surfaces in several inflections should say so rather than assert a single canonical string.
  - **A row you just corrected can still be half-wrong — re-verify every column, not the one the finding pointed at (2026-09-04).** A further post-merge round on the same book found that the fix above only went halfway: having established that the "rend asunder" row's *Portuguese* column named a string absent from the chapter, the round corrected that column and closed — without asking the same question of the row's *English* column, which named "rend asunder", a string that occurs nowhere in `en-US/ch001.md` either (the source has "rent asunder" in the narrative and bare "rend" in the sound-alike list). A Key Terms row is one claim about a correspondence between two texts; a finding against either half is reason to re-check both halves, and the grep that verifies it must run over both files. The general shape: **a defect found in an artifact is evidence about the process that produced it, not just about the one line it surfaced on** — the same inattention that let a false Portuguese string in wrote the English string beside it.
  - **Corollary on cold monolingual passes re-raising a settled item.** Round 7's cold reader independently flagged the number mismatch in "perguntou a cada um, isoladamente, onde a haviam visto" — the same item round 5 raised and rejected, because the en-US has the identical mismatch ("asked them severally where **they saw** her"). Two independent cold readers converging on the same construction is the *expected* signature of a faithful mirror of an odd source, not mounting evidence of a defect; the test is still whether the source has it, and re-litigating a rejection that already cites the source line wastes the round. Recording rejected items *with their source citation* in the translation journal is what makes the next round cheap — the reviewer can confirm the rejection in one grep instead of re-deriving it.
- **Origen's letter *Origen to Gregory* (Crombie translation) translated to pt-BR (2026-09-04).** ~1,073 words, single chapter, 3 numbered paragraphs — Origen urging Gregory Thaumaturgus to use Greek philosophy and the sciences as a preparation for Christian scriptural study, with the Exodus "spoiling the Egyptians" allegory and the Ader/Bethel/Dan digression on heresy. Picked from the `church-fathers/` backlog (still ~400+ English-only works). Reused the "Orígenes" pt-BR author form already established by the sibling `africanus-to-origen` translation. Six review rounds (bilingual/monolingual alternating), five genuine defects found and fixed, closing on two consecutive clean rounds (5, 6).
  - **The corpus catalogues this same letter twice, under two different New Advent translations.** `church-fathers/origen/origen-to-gregory` (Crombie, ANF Vol. 4, `0415.htm`) and `church-fathers/origen/letter-of-origen-to-gregory` (Menzies, ANF Vol. 9, `1014.htm`) are the same historical letter — different 19th-century translators, catalogued as two separate `book.json` units with different ids. Only the Crombie one got a pt-BR translation this pass; the Menzies one is still English-only and untouched. Not fixed or deduplicated here — flagging for whoever next surveys the `church-fathers/` backlog for duplicates, since a translator picking `letter-of-origen-to-gregory` next would otherwise not notice it's the same work already available bilingually under a different id.
  - **A hypothetical "if X, [conditional] Y" reads correctly in Portuguese only with `se` + imperfect subjunctive, not `se` + imperfect indicative** — en-US's own loose "if there was a third and fourth quality of gold, from it would be made the holy vessels" needs `se houvesse... seriam feitos`, not `se havia... seriam feitos`, even though the source's "was" is indicative; Portuguese grammaticalizes the hypothetical mood that English leaves to context.
  - **A genuinely ambiguous English pronoun ("his wife's sister") can be resolved by the underlying Bible narrative, but the target language then has to make that resolution stick grammatically, not just semantically.** "His" in "made a kinsman of Pharaoh by marrying his wife's sister" is ambiguous in English between Ader and Pharaoh, but 1 Kings 11:19-20 settles it (Pharaoh gave Ader the sister of Pharaoh's own wife). A literal `sua mulher` in Portuguese doesn't inherit English's resolvable ambiguity — Portuguese anaphora defaults to the nearest antecedent (the subject, Ader), producing a reading the source doesn't have. Fixed with the demonstrative `deste`, which unambiguously points to the immediately preceding noun (Faraó).
  - **Two cold monolingual readers, two rounds apart, independently re-raised the same two items** (a singular pronoun "dela" resuming a two-part "third and fourth quality" antecedent, and the antecedent of a sentence-final "que fez isso" after an intervening relative clause) — both already checked against the source and rejected in round 4 (en-US itself uses a singular "from it," and has the identical long-distance "It was... that he did this" correlative). Consistent with the corpus's standing rule: convergent cold-reader flags on a construction that mirrors the source are confirmation of fidelity, not new evidence — the test stays whether the source has it, not how many readers stumble on it.
  - **Post-merge audit of the same book (round 7) found three more defects — the pre-merge rounds' blind spot was *inside the noun phrase* (2026-09-04).** Six rounds of clause-level bilingual and monolingual review closed on two clean rounds, and a seventh round still found real defects. All three sat below the clause level the earlier rounds were scanning at:
    - **English stacks modifiers in an order Portuguese can silently re-bracket.** "The solid golden candlestick" means a candlestick of *solid gold*; `candelabro sólido de ouro` attaches *sólido* to *candelabro* and *de ouro* separately, yielding "the solid candlestick of gold" — a different object description that still reads as fluent Portuguese, which is why six clause-level rounds walked past it. Fixed to `candelabro de ouro maciço`. Portuguese also collocates solid metal as *maciço*, never *sólido* (which reads as firm/robust, or as a state of matter). **Adjective-stack noun phrases in the source deserve their own pass: re-derive what modifies what before checking the words.**
    - **A displaced adverb changes what a sentence hedges without changing any of its words.** en-US "Ader at least, the Idumæan; so long as he was in the land of Israel... made no idols" fences "at least" off from the temporal clause, so it hedges the *exemplar* ("Ader, for one"). The pt-BR had `Ader, o idumeu, ao menos enquanto esteve...` — with no comma after *ao menos*, it scopes over the *timeframe* instead ("at least while he was..."). Same words, same propositional content, different claim shape. A translation-completeness check that counts negations, quotations, and proper names cannot see this; only re-deriving the source's own constituent order can. Fixed by restoring that order: `Ader ao menos, o idumeu, enquanto...`.
    - **The Key Terms journal-audit rule now has a third instance, and it keeps landing on the same book.** A row's English column named `Ader, the Idumæan`, a comma-form string in neither file (the source has "Ader at least, the Idumæan" and bare "Ader the Idumæan"). This is the third time a Key Terms row has asserted a string absent from the text it claims to describe. **The grep-both-columns check is cheap enough to be unconditional, and a row whose term surfaces in several forms should enumerate them rather than invent a canonical one** — the row now names both attested forms in both languages.
  - **A ninth round on the same book found three more — and the blind spot had moved again, this time to constructions that are grammatical *English* wearing Portuguese words (2026-09-04).** Round 7's post-merge audit closed on two clean rounds (7, 8); a round-9 audit after PR #402 still found three real defects. Where rounds 1–6 scanned at the clause level and rounds 7–8 dropped inside the noun phrase, all three of these are **anglicisms at the level of grammatical licensing** — each is a construction English permits and Portuguese does not, so each reads as fluent Portuguese to anyone whose parse is being steered by the English sitting beside it in a bilingual pass:
    - **English promotes indirect objects to passive subjects; Portuguese cannot.** en-US "the children of Israel **were commanded** to ask" became `os filhos de Israel **foram ordenados** a pedir`. Portuguese *ordenar* governs the person as a dative (*ordenar a alguém que faça*), and a Portuguese passive can only promote the direct object — so the person commanded can never be the passive subject, exactly as "*fui dito", "*fui perguntado", "*fui dado" are all ungrammatical. The trap is that *ser ordenado* **is** a licensed passive with an unrelated sense (ordained a priest; sorted), so the sentence had a real but wrong reading — "the children of Israel were *ordained* to ask" — rather than reading as broken. Fixed to `aos filhos de Israel foi ordenado que pedissem`. **The corpus already knew the answer**: 49 `ordenou a X` dative instances across `content/books/*/pt-BR` (plus the impersonal `foi ordenado que` forms), against exactly one `X foram ordenados a` + infinitive anywhere in the corpus — this file's. A one-line grep for the verb's government across the corpus would have caught it in any of the eight prior rounds.
    - **English notional plurals ("people... them") have no Portuguese counterpart.** en-US "divide the people of God, and to make **them** say" became `dividir o povo de Deus, e para **fazê-los** dizer`. *Povo* is grammatically singular masculine, so the plural clitic had no antecedent in its own sentence. Fixed to `fazê-lo`. Corpus confirms twice over, including on this exact pericope: `montfort-true-devotion` has the identical causative (`sobre o povo, a fim de **fazê-lo** entrar`), and `liguori-apparecchio-alla-morte` narrates *this very Jeroboam/golden-calf scene* with singular clitics (`o povo... **lhe** apresentou os seus ídolos e **lhe** disse`).
    - **The pretérito perfeito composto is a false friend of the English present perfect.** `têm perdido` obligatorily means an action iterated or continuing up to now; en-US "which so many **have missed**" reports a completed fact about many past readers. Fixed to `que tantos perderam`. The tell was available without leaving the file: this was the chapter's **only** composto, and it stood two sentences from the translator's own correct simple-past renderings of the same English construction ("have set up" → *erigiram*, "have become" → *Tornamo-nos*). **An aspect marker that appears exactly once in a text whose source uses that construction repeatedly is a defect signature, not a stylistic choice** — worth a mechanical check: count composto forms per file and compare against the source's present-perfect count.
    - **The generalizable finding across rounds 7 and 9: a bilingual reviewer's parse of the target text is contaminated by the source sitting next to it.** Every defect these two rounds found is invisible precisely *because* the English is there — the reader's grammar for the sentence comes from the English, so a Portuguese string that maps word-for-word onto a licensed English construction gets parsed as licensed. The cold monolingual pass exists to break that, but it did not catch these either, because each string is locally well-formed; what breaks it is the **corpus grep**: ask how the rest of `content/books/*/pt-BR` conjugates, governs, or agrees with this exact word, and the outlier surfaces mechanically. That check found the answer for all three defects here, and it needs no bilingual judgment at all.
    - **A reviewer's own corpus claim is a claim to verify — same rule, new target (2026-09-04).** Round 10's bilingual pass reported the pt-BR file's straight quotes as a defect against a corpus convention of curly quotes, citing "38 other chapter files under `content/books/church-fathers/`". Round 7 had surveyed this and concluded the opposite. Re-counting settled it for round 7: there are only **21** pt-BR chapter files under `church-fathers/` at all, 4 of them quote-free, and **of the 17 that quote, 13 are straight and 4 curly**. The reviewer's number came from counting the whole tree (overwhelmingly `en-US`) rather than pt-BR chapters — a plausible-looking figure attached to a real directory, which is exactly what makes it persuasive. This book has now been the site of four Key Terms rows asserting unattested strings, one asserting a wrong count, and now a review finding asserting a wrong corpus survey. **Any claim of the form "the corpus does X" — whether it comes from a translation journal, a reviewer, or a prior round — gets re-derived with a script before it drives a change**, and the script counts the population the claim is actually about.
    - **Round 11 found nothing in the text, and the reason is worth more than the finding: a corpus search that returns *zero* has to be validated against its own target string (2026-09-04).** Both defects proposed this round died on re-measurement, and each failed in a different way. (a) The bilingual pass called `prosseguir … a expor` an unlicensed calque of "go on … to set forth," reporting **zero** corpus instances of `prosseguir a` + infinitive against 64 for `continuar a` + infinitive. But its regex required the infinitive to sit immediately after `a`, so it could not have matched the target string either — the sentence has a comma-fenced parenthetical in between. Widening it surfaced a real independent attestation (`liguori-glorie-di-maria`: *para prosseguir ali sempre a louvar a sua benfeitora*, adverbs interposed the same way), and `prosseguir` turns out to be an ordinary member of the aspectual class that licenses `a` + infinitive (*continuar a*, *passar a*, *voltar a*). **A zero result is a claim about a regex before it is a claim about the corpus: run it against the string you are accusing, and if it does not match that either, the zero means nothing.** (b) The mechanical pass flagged `indispensável ao` as the file's one outlier, claiming 20 of 20 corpus instances govern *para* and none `a`/`ao` — there are 7 with `a`/`ao` (`indispensáveis à vida`, `ao uso`, `à existência`, three more in `liguori-meditacoes`), and the control adjective *necessário* has 80. **Both reviewers reported a confident negative from a search that was simply too narrow to find the counterexample** — which is the failure mode a negative corpus result is most prone to, and the mirror image of the round-10 lesson about inflated positive counts.
    - **The one durable check across all eleven rounds is the cold *monolingual* reader — not for what they flag, but for what they decline to flag.** Round 9's anglicisms slipped past monolingual readers because each was locally well-formed; that is a real limit. But the converse held decisively here: the monolingual reader, with no English in front of them, read `prosseguir … a expor` and judged it grammatical-if-formal. An unlicensed calque reads as *broken* to an uncontaminated reader. So the two signals compose into a usable test — **corpus grep proposes, cold monolingual reading disposes**: a construction that greps as an outlier *and* reads as broken to a source-free reader is a defect (rounds 7, 9); one that greps as an outlier but reads as merely formal is a register choice (rounds 7 *tais como*, 11 *prosseguir a*).
    - **Round 12 sharpened the zero-result rule: validating your regex against the string you are accusing is necessary but not sufficient — it must also be able to match the construction's other instances (2026-09-04).** The round-12 bilingual pass called `a oração é, de todas as coisas, indispensável` a calque of the English intensifier "of all things," reporting exactly 1 corpus match: the accused sentence. It had followed the round-11 rule and confirmed its pattern matched the target. But the pattern fixed the *noun* — `de todas as **coisas**` — so no counterexample with any other noun could ever have appeared, and the "1 match" was an artifact of the search, not a fact about Portuguese. Testing the *construction class* instead (`, de todos os/todas as X,` with any noun) found it attested **16** times across five books, plus 6 fronted partitives (`De todas as nações, Deus escolheu uma`), three of them the accused sentence's exact copula + interposed-partitive + predicate shape — `liguori-pratica-di-amar-gesu-cristo`'s `é a devoção, de todas as devoções, a mais útil` and two in `trent-catechism`. **A search that can only match the string you are accusing has a guaranteed answer before you run it. Abstract one slot — the noun, the adverb, the interposed material — and re-run: if the construction has a family, search for the family.**
    - **The cold monolingual reader's *silence* is evidence, and it accumulates across rounds.** Four separate source-free readers (rounds 4, 6, 10, 11) have now read the `de todas as coisas` sentence and none flagged it, exactly as none flagged `prosseguir … a expor`. Both were nonetheless proposed as anglicisms by bilingual passes, which see the English beside the Portuguese and read the parallel as evidence of calquing. The asymmetry is usable: **a bilingual pass is the right instrument for finding a calque and the wrong one for confirming it**, because the source it needs in order to notice the parallel is the same thing that makes the parallel look damning. Confirmation belongs to the corpus grep and the source-free reader.
    - **A count you re-derive correctly must be written back over the claim that was wrong, not just recorded alongside it.** Round 7 logged the pt-BR quote survey as "14 of 17 straight"; round 10 re-derived it as 13 straight / 4 curly and wrote that down as a *new* entry, leaving round 7's figure standing in both this file and the book's translation journal. The two disagreed for three rounds, and round 11 had to re-measure from scratch to find out which was right (13/4 — round 10). **A correction that does not overwrite the original claim leaves the codebase asserting both numbers**, and every later round pays the re-derivation cost again. Fixed in both journals, with the correction noted at the original site. Round 12 found two more of the same shape in this book's journal — round 7's "all 10 negations" (it is 9 in both files; the 10th is an artifact of counting pt-BR *tokens*, since en-US `lest` is rendered with both `não` and `nada`), and a Translation Decisions bullet that opened "The **two** Scripture quotations" and then listed **three** in its own parenthetical (there are 5, plus a non-Scripture gloss).
    - **Record the pattern next to the count, or the count is not re-checkable — it is only re-derivable, which is the expensive kind (2026-09-04).** Round 11 logged a corpus control figure as "*necessário* + `ao`/`à` appears 80 times against 333 with `para`." Round 12, re-deriving it, got 69 and flagged the number as suspect. Both were right: round 11's regex included the plurals `às`/`aos`, round 12's did not. **A bare number in a journal silently encodes a population choice**, and the next round cannot tell a wrong figure from a differently-scoped one without guessing at the original query. The figure was correct and the recording was not. Every corpus count in this book's journal now carries the pattern that produces it, which turns a re-derivation into a one-command re-check — the cheapest possible form of the verify-every-claim rule this book keeps re-learning.
    - **Round 13: "record the pattern" is necessary and still not sufficient — a reproducible count needs its flags, its population, and one pass of eyeballing (2026-09-04).** Round 13 re-derived all ten figures rounds 11 and 12 had just written down. Seven verified exactly; **the three that failed were all counts this audit had itself produced while writing the rule about recording counts.** (a) `necessário` + `a`-article = 80 only with `re.IGNORECASE`; the regex as recorded gives 78, losing `necessárias à Igreja` and `necessária à Igreja` to a lowercase-only character class. That number had *already* been repaired once, for a different unstated scope choice (plurals `às`/`aos`). **The flags are part of the pattern.** (b) The fronted-partitive count was 6 and is 7 — the missed instance opens a Markdown blockquote (`\n\n> De todos os vícios,`), so any "sentence-initial" anchor on `^` or on a preceding `.` is blind to it. **Markdown line prefixes are part of the corpus; a search that only sees prose paragraphs is not searching the corpus.** (c) `continuar … a` + infinitive = 43 is exactly reproducible and contains one false positive: `recusam continuar trabalhando, para forçar o empregador a ceder`, where `continuar` takes a gerund and the `a` belongs to `forçar`. The finding survived (42 vs 2 says what 43 vs 2 said), but **a mechanically-exact count nobody read through still had a wrong member in it.**
    - **An audit that writes into the corpus it searches will start counting itself.** Round 13 measured `indispensável` + `a`-article as 7 or **12**, depending only on whether `translation-journal.md` files are in the population — because the journal's own prose, discussing the construction, now lives under `content/books/**/pt-BR/`. This book's journal has grown long enough to move a small corpus figure by itself. **Declare the population with every count** (here: chapter files only, excluding translation journals), and treat any corpus statistic about a construction the journal has been arguing about as polluted until proven otherwise.
    - **The Key Terms rule generalizes from strings to *claims*.** The Ader row — fixed in round 7 for naming an unattested string — was still asserting "the name surfaces twice in each file." The *Idumæan formula* surfaces twice; the name surfaces three times, the third inside "brethren of Ader"/"irmãos de Ader". Fourth instance of this row family being wrong. The rule is no longer "grep both columns" but **"every falsifiable claim in a translation journal is a claim to verify, counts included"** — and a row corrected once is not thereby correct.
  - **Check a translation's punctuation convention against the corpus before "fixing" it.** Round 7 flagged the pt-BR file's straight quotes against its own en-US source's curly quotes, and against the curly quotes of its sibling `origen/africanus-to-origen`. Surveying all 17 quoted `church-fathers/*/pt-BR` chapters settled it the other way: 13 use straight quotes and 4 curly, so the file already followed the dominant convention and the sibling is the outlier. (Round 7 originally recorded this as "14 use straight"; the entry two bullets up re-derived 13/4, and round 11 re-measured and corrected the figure here — the two entries had disagreed for three rounds.) Rejected, no change. Two related gotchas worth recording: the pt-BR/en-US quote-character *counts* still have to match (12/12 here — that check catches dropped quotations regardless of style), and `grep -o '[“”]'` under a non-UTF-8 locale matches individual bytes, so it silently counts em dashes and apostrophes too — count quote characters in Python, not with a shell bracket expression.
- **Tatian's *Fragments* translated to pt-BR (2026-09-05).** ~800 words, single chapter, 12 numbered fragments — not a continuous work by Tatian but a collection of excerpts *about* him (Clement of Alexandria, Jerome, Irenaeus, Origen, and Archelaus via Routh's *Reliquiæ*, quoting or refuting his Encratite positions on marriage, wine, and the Old Testament). Picked from the `church-fathers/` backlog (still ~398 English-only works after this pass) as a well-scoped single-work pick. Each fragment's trailing citation (e.g. "— Clem. Alex.: *Strom.*, iii. c. 12.") was kept in its original Latin/scholarly abbreviated form rather than translated — it's bibliographic apparatus, not prose. Rendered with straight quotes per the corpus's dominant `church-fathers/*/pt-BR` convention (see the entry above) rather than the source's curly quotes.
  - Five review rounds (structural/mechanical, bilingual fidelity, monolingual fresh-eyes, full re-verification, mechanical corpus-tooling), closing on two consecutive clean rounds (4, 5). Genuine defects fixed: two citation-formatting drifts from the source's own italics/whitespace inconsistencies (fixing a translation slip, not "correcting" the source), a mistranslated intensifier that became a superlative ("very violent" → wrongly "o mais violento," corrected to "o violentíssimo"), a misattributed conceit (English's ambiguous "a conceit of his teacher" wrongly resolved to the *teacher's* vanity instead of Tatian's own), a softened punitive doublet ("chastised" → "corrected," restored), and three Portuguese-specific readability fixes (a relative-pronoun antecedent worsened by grammatical-gender mismatch, a calqued word order, a resumptive-pronoun clause a fresh reader misparsed as a double subject).
- **John Chrysostom's *Letter to Some Priests of Antioch* translated to pt-BR (2026-09-05).** ~545 words, single chapter, one unbroken paragraph — a personal letter to Castus, Valerius, Diophantus, and Cyriacus, presbyters of Antioch, built entirely on an extended debt/payment conceit for the mutual obligations of love. Picked from the `church-fathers/` backlog after excluding several deceptively tiny "0 KB" entries (`gospel-of-nicodemus`, `gregory-nazianzen/letters`, `ephraim/nisibene-hymns`) that turned out to be bare TOC/division stubs, not translatable chapters — worth flagging for whoever next sorts the backlog by word count, since a naive word-count sort surfaces these ahead of every real short work.
  - Seven review rounds, closing on two consecutive clean rounds (6, 7) — three rounds each found and fixed a genuine defect, one clean round sat between two dirty ones and had to be re-run. **The costliest single sentence was "For he who pays it is not less bereft of it... but payment of love makes him who pays richer than before," edited in three separate rounds (1, 3, 5) before it stabilized**, which is itself the finding worth recording:
    1. **Round 1 — an archaic elliptical comparative read as a flat negation, inverting the sentence's point.** "Not less bereft of it, as in the case of money" is short for "not less bereft of it **than** [happens] in the case of money" — asserting the payer of love loses it *fully*, exactly as a payer of money does; the paradox the sentence sets up is that this loss nonetheless enriches rather than impoverishes him. First draft read "not less bereft" as "not bereft at all" and translated the opposite claim ("não fica dela privado"). Fixed to preserve the comparative: "não fica **menos** privado dela **do que** sucede no caso do dinheiro."
    2. **Round 3 — the fix itself introduced an internal pronoun-gender mismatch.** The round-1 rewrite used feminine `a`/`dela` (agreeing with *dívida*, established two sentences earlier) in the first clause but masculine `o` in the second clause's translator-added object pronoun ("aquele que **o** paga") — same antecedent, disagreeing genders in one sentence. The object pronoun wasn't in the source to begin with ("him who pays" is used absolutely in English); removed it rather than resolve its gender.
    3. **Round 5 — the now-grammatical sentence still invited a misparse from its word order.** "Torna mais rico do que antes aquele que paga" fronts the predicate ahead of its postposed object, so a cold reader parses "mais rico do que antes" as a complete comparison and then has to backtrack onto the dangling "aquele que paga." Reordered to plain SVO — "torna aquele que paga mais rico do que antes" — which happens to also be the English's own word order.
  - **A fix round is not self-certifying — the very next round should target exactly what the last round touched, not the whole file again.** Rounds 3 and 5 each found their defect by re-deriving the prior round's edit from scratch rather than trusting it because it had just been "fixed." Round 6 made this explicit policy: after three edits to one sentence, it re-verified that sentence specifically (grammaticality, negation shape, crase/contraction correctness) before declaring the round clean — and only then did two rounds in a row come back clean.
  - **A Key Terms row's supporting evidence needs the same grep as its claim.** The first-draft journal asserted "aliança" for "covenant" "match[ed] the corpus's standard rendering... elsewhere" with no citation. A corpus grep in round 4 found the real pattern is a semantic split, not a blanket standard: `aliança` for the religious/biblical sense (`Arca da aliança`, `aliança com a morte` quoting Isaiah) and `pacto` for a secular one (`pacto de um amor luxurioso`); the word choice was already correct, but the row's justification wasn't checked until this round.
  - **A fragment collection's own verbless sentences are not translation defects, and a monolingual reviewer will flag them as though they were.** Six of round 3's ten flags were sentence-fragments, undelimited inline citations, or unresolved plural referents that the *English source itself* has — this chapter is literally titled "Fragments" because it excerpts testimonia mid-thought. A reviewer reading only the Portuguese has no way to know a verbless sentence is source-faithful rather than a dropped verb; every such flag had to be checked against the English before deciding whether to fix or reject: six of the ten were rejected as source-faithful, and the other four survived that check as genuinely worth fixing anyway (the same fragmentary shape read strictly worse in Portuguese than in English, e.g. a relative pronoun losing its only disambiguating cue when the source's adjacent-noun-gender trick doesn't carry over).
